\mathbf{x}_{l}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}(x_{0},j_{\mathbf{x},0}(l))\\
\vdots\\
(x_{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)-1},j_{\mathbf{x},i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)-1}(l))\\
(x_{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)},j_{\mathbf{x},i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)}(l-1))\\
(x_{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)},j_{\mathbf{x},i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)}(l))\\
(x_{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)+1},j_{\mathbf{x},i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)+1}(l))\\
\vdots\\
(x_{p},j_{\mathbf{x},p}(l))\end{pmatrix}\in(X\times I)^{p+2},

that is, the entries are exactly the pairs
(x_{i},j_{\mathbf{x},i}(l))=(x_{i},j_{\mathbf{x},i}(l-1))
for
i\not=i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)
and together with the two pairs

(x_{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)},j_{\mathbf{x},i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)}(l-1))\not=(x_{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)},j_{\mathbf{x},i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)}(l))
.

The construction was made in such a way that if
\mathbf{x}
lies in a
E
-penumbra of the multidiagonal in
X^{p+1}
, then
\mathbf{x}_{l}
lies in a
E_{\mathcal{U}}^{-1}\circ(E\mathbin{\bar{\times}}{\Delta_{[a,b]}})\circ E_{\mathcal{U}}
-penumbra of the multidiagonal in
(X\times I)^{p+2}
.
Note also, that this construction is clearly
\Gamma
-equivariant thanks to the invariant choice of the
s_{x,j}
: We have
k_{\mathbf{x}\gamma}=k_{\mathbf{x}}
and
(\mathbf{x}\gamma)_{l}=(\mathbf{x}_{l})\gamma
for each
l=1,\dots,k_{\mathbf{x}}
.

The prism operator for the given
\Gamma
-equivariant generalized coarse homotopy is now defined as

\displaystyle P_{H}\colon\ \mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X,A;M)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p+1}(Y,B;M),

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in X^{p+1}}m_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in X^{p+1}}m_{\mathbf{x}}\sum_{l=1}^{k_{\mathbf{x}}}(-1)^{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)}H_{*}(\mathbf{x}_{l}).

Because of the above-mentioned properties, the image of this map indeed consists of
\Gamma
-invariant coarse chains. A calculation similar to the one for the classical prism operator known from basic algebraic topology (cf.
[
18
, proof of Theorem 2.10]
) now shows that
P_{H}
is a chain homotopy between
f_{*}
and
g_{*}
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

The ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse homology satisfies the excision axiom.

Proof.

We first show surjectivity of the map
\mathrm{HX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;M)\to\mathrm{HX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X,A;M)
. An element of the target is represented by a
\Gamma
-equivariant chain
c\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X;M)
with
\partial c\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p-1}(A;M)
. As
\supp(c)
is a
\Gamma
-invariant penumbra of the multidiagonal, the set

\displaystyle E\coloneqq\{(y,z)\in X\times X\mid\exists(x_{0},\dots,x_{p})\in\supp(c),\,\exists i,j\in\{0,\dots,p\}\colon x_{i}=y\wedge x_{j}=z\}\cup{\Delta_{X}}

is a
\Gamma
-invariant entourage which contains the diagonal. In particular, all
E
-penumbras
\Pen_{E}(B)
of
\Gamma
-invariant subspaces
B
are also
\Gamma
-invariant, and we have included the diagonal into
E
to ensure that
\Pen_{E}(B)\supset B
.

Then the chain
c
can be decomposed as
c=c_{1}+c_{2}
with
c_{2}
being the
\Gamma
-equivariant chain in
\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(\Pen_{E}(A);M)
consisting of all those summands of
c
which are supported in
\Pen_{E}(A)^{p+1}
, and therefore
c_{1}\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus A;M)\subset\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus C;M)
.
We have

\displaystyle\partial c_{1}=\partial c-\partial c_{2}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p-1}(X\setminus C;M)\cap\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p-1}(\Pen_{E}(A);M)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p-1}(\Pen_{E}(A)\setminus C;M)

and hence
c_{1}
represents a class in

\mathrm{HX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus C,\Pen_{E}(A)\setminus C;M)

which is clearly mapped to the class of
c
in
\mathrm{HX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X,A;M)
under the inclusion of pairs of coarse
\Gamma
-spaces.

Now the surjectivity of the excision map follows by noting that the inclusion
(X\setminus C,A\setminus C)\subset(X\setminus C,\Pen_{E}(A)\setminus C)
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse equivalence and evoking the preceeding lemma. A coarse inverse up to closeness is obtained as follows: The excisiveness condition gives us an entourage
F
such that

\Pen_{E}(A)\setminus C\subset\Pen_{F}(A\setminus C)
.
Note that by replacing
F
with
\bigcap_{\gamma\in\Gamma}F\gamma
, if necessary, we can assume that
F
is
\Gamma
-invariant. This works, because
E
is
\Gamma
-invariant. The coarse inverse can now be defined as being the identity on
X\setminus\Pen_{E}(A)
and mapping
\Pen_{E}(A)\setminus C
to
A\setminus C
in a way which is
F
-close to the identity and
\Gamma
-equivariant.

In order to show injectivity, we start with
c\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus C;M)
with
\partial c\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p-1}(A\setminus C;M)
for which there is
d\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p+1}(X;M)
such that
\partial d-c\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(A;M)
, because this is equivalent to
c
representing an element of the kernel. Decomposing
d
into
d_{1}\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p+1}(X\setminus C;M)
and
d_{2}\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p+1}(\Pen_{E}(A);M)
as above, where this time
E
is obtained from the support of
d
, we see that

\displaystyle\partial d_{1}-c=\partial d-c-\partial d_{2}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus C;M)\cap\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(\Pen_{E}(A);M)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(\Pen_{E}(A)\setminus C;M).

This means that the class of
c
is mapped to zero under

\mathrm{HX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;M)\to\mathrm{HX}^{\Gamma}_{p}(X\setminus C,\Pen_{E}(A)\setminus C;M),

but we have already seen above that the latter map is an isomorphism. The claim follows.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

The ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse homology satisfies the flasqueness axiom.

Proof.

If
(X,A)
is a flasque coarse
\Gamma
-space with
\Gamma
-equivariant
\phi
as in Definitions
2.14
and
2.23
, then a chain contraction is given by
the
M
-linear extension
\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X,A;M)\to\mathrm{CX}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}(X,A;M)
of the mapping

(x_{0},\dots,x_{p})\mapsto\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{i}\big(\phi^{n+1}(x_{0}),\dots,\phi^{n+1}(x_{i}),\phi^{n}(x_{i}),\dots,\phi^{n}(x_{i})\big).

The sums are finite on all relatively compact
K^{p+1}
, because the image of
\phi^{n}
does not intersect
K
for
n
large enough and
\phi
is close to the identity. Furthermore, equicontrolledness of the
\phi^{n}
together with closeness of
\phi
to
\id
ensure that the support of
c
is a penumbra of the multidiagonal.
∎

4.2
Ordinary coarse cohomology

Roe defined coarse cohomology in
[
35
]
only for metric spaces without group action and with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}
.
We generalize the definition below in the obvious way.

It is a bit surprising and disappointing that, although ordinary coarse cohomology is arguably the most elementary coarse cohomology theory whose groups can be defined on all coarse
\Gamma
-spaces, we nevertheless have to restrict ourselves to countably generated coarsely connected isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces in order to be able to prove the excision axiom.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
X
be a coarse space and
M
an abelian group. For
p\in\mathbb{N}
we define
\mathrm{CX}^{p}(X;M)
as the group of all functions
\varphi\colon X^{p+1}\to M
whose support
\supp(\varphi)
intersects each penumbra of the multidiagonal in a relatively compact subset.
For
p\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathbb{N}
we define
\mathrm{CX}^{p}(X;M)\coloneqq 0
.
These groups together with the Alexander–Spanier coboundary maps
\delta\colon\mathrm{CX}^{p}(X;M)\to\mathrm{CX}^{p+1}(X;M)
, that is

\delta\varphi(x_{0},\dots,x_{p+1})\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{p+1}(-1)^{i}\varphi(x_{0},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{p+1}),

constitute a cochain complex.
If
A\subset X
is a coarse subspace, then we define the cochain complex
\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X,A;M)
as the kernel of the surjective cochain map
\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X;M)\to\mathrm{CX}^{*}(A;M)
and its cohomology is the
ordinary coarse cohomology

\mathrm{HX}^{*}(X,A;M)
of the pair
(X,A)
with coefficients in
M
.

If
\Gamma
is a discrete group acting isocoarsely on
(X,A)
from the right and acting on
M
from the left, then it also acts on the cochain complex
\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X,A;M)
from the left via
(\gamma\varphi)(x_{0},\dots,x_{p})\allowbreak\coloneqq\gamma(\varphi(x_{0}\gamma,\dots,x_{p}\gamma))
. Hence we have a quotient cochain complex

\mathrm{CX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X,A;M)\coloneqq\linebreak\Gamma\backslash\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X,A;M)
,
i.e., we divide out the subcomplexes generated by
\gamma\varphi-\varphi
for all
\gamma
,
\varphi
, and we call its cohomology the
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse cohomology
\mathrm{HX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X,A;M)
.

Ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse cohomology is clearly contravariantly functorial under
\Gamma
-equivariant maps between pairs of coarse
\Gamma
-spaces.

Lemma 4.8
.

The ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse cohomology satisfies the exactness axiom.

Proof.

Again, this is implied by the fact that the short exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X,A;M)\to\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X;M)\to\mathrm{CX}^{*}(A;M)\to 0

admits a
\Gamma
-equivariant split
\mathrm{CX}^{*}(A;M)\to\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X;M)
, defined by extending cocycles by
0
on all
(p+1)
-tuples not supported within
A^{p+1}
, and hence the quotient complexes also form a short exact sequence.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

The ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse cohomology satisfies the strong homotopy axiom. In particular, it is also contravariantly functorial under
closeness classes
of coarse maps.

Proof.

The proof is essentially dual to the proof of Lemma
4.4
. Using exactly the same
k_{\mathbf{x}}
,
i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)
and
\mathbf{x}_{l}
constructed from an arbitrary
\mathbf{x}\in X^{p+1}
we can define the cochain homotopy

\displaystyle P_{H}\colon\ \mathrm{CX}_{\Gamma}^{p+1}(X,A;M)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{CX}_{\Gamma}^{p}(X,A;M),

\displaystyle P_{H}(\varphi)(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{l=0}^{k_{\mathbf{x}}}(-1)^{i_{\mathbf{x}}(l)}\varphi(H_{*}(\mathbf{x}_{l})).

It does the job.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

On the admissible category of pairs of countably generated coarsely connected isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces the ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse homology satisfies the excision axiom.

Proof.

Assume for a moment that there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant map
r\colon X\to X\setminus C
which restricts to the identity on
X\setminus C
, maps
C
(and hence also
A
) into
A\setminus C
and restricts to a controlled map on
\Pen_{E}(X\setminus C)
for every penumbra
E
of
X
.
We claim that
r
then induces an inverse to the excision homomorphism on the cohomology groups.

Let
\varphi\in\mathrm{CX}^{p}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;M)
and
P
be a penumbra of the multidiagonal in
X^{p+1}
. Note that the latter is equivalent to
P
being contained in
\bigcup_{x\in X}(\Pen_{E}(\{x\}))^{p+1}
for some entourage
E
of
X
.
Hence, if one component
x_{i}
of
\mathbf{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{p})\in P
is not contained in
\Pen_{E\circ E^{-1}}(X\setminus C)
, then none of the components is contained in
X\setminus C
. In particular, all components of
\mathbf{x}
are mapped to
A\setminus C
by
r
, implying
r^{p+1}(\mathbf{x})\in(A\setminus C)^{p+1}
and therefore
r^{*}\varphi(\mathbf{x})=0
.
This shows
\supp(r^{*}\varphi)\cap P\subset(\Pen_{E\circ E^{-1}}(X\setminus C))^{p+1}
. By assumption, the restriction of
r
to
\Pen_{E\circ E^{-1}}(X\setminus C)
is a controlled map. Together with the other assumption on
r
we see that it is close to the identity on
\Pen_{E\circ E^{-1}}(X\setminus C)
and therefore must also be proper, that is, it is even a coarse map. Now, controlledness of
r
implies that
r^{p+1}(\supp(r^{*}\varphi)\cap P)
is a penumbra of the multidiagonal in
X\setminus C
. In addition, it is also contained in
\supp(\varphi)
and hence it is relatively compact due to the support condition on
\varphi
. Exploiting that
r
is also proper we conclude that
\supp(r^{*}\varphi)\cap P
is relatively compact.

We have just shown that
r^{*}
maps
\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;M)
to
\mathrm{CX}^{*}(X,A;M)
. The map is clearly compatible with the group action and the coboundary map. Therefore it induces a map
r^{*}\colon\mathrm{HX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;M)\to\mathrm{HX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X,A;M)
on cohomology and we claim that it is an inverse to the excision map. One of the two compositions is trivially the identity, because
r
restricts to the identity on
X\setminus C
. The other composition is homotopic to the identity on cochain level via the “prism operator”

P(\varphi)(x_{0},\dots,x_{p-1})\coloneqq\sum_{l=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{l}\varphi(x_{0},\dots,x_{l},r(x_{l}),\dots,r(x_{p-1})),

where the proof that this formula really defines a map
\mathrm{CX}_{\Gamma}^{p}(X,A;M)\to\mathrm{CX}_{\Gamma}^{p-1}(X,A;M)
is completely analogous to the above proof that
r^{*}
maps
\mathrm{CX}_{\Gamma}^{p}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;M)
to
\mathrm{CX}_{\Gamma}^{p}(X,A;M)
.

It remains to prove that the map
r
exists. If
X
is a countably generated isocoarse
\Gamma
-space, then we can pick a sequence
{\Delta_{X}}=E_{0}\subset E_{1}\subset E_{2}\subset\cdots
of
\Gamma
-invariant entourages such that every entourage of
X
is contained in one of the
E_{n}
. Using the alternative excisiveness condition (
3.1
) we find for each
E_{n}
an entourage
F_{n}
such that
A\cap\Pen_{E_{n}}(X\setminus C)\subset\Pen_{F_{n}}(A\setminus C)
. We can then define
r
by patching together the identity on
X\setminus C=\Pen_{E_{0}}(X\setminus C)
with maps

\Pen_{E_{n}}(X\setminus C)\setminus\Pen_{E_{n-1}}(X\setminus C)\to A\setminus C

that are
F_{n}
-close to the identity. The assumption of coarse connectedness ensures that this defines
r
on the whole space
X
. All the above-mentioned assumptions on
r
are readily verified.
∎

Finally, the flasqueness axiom is proven by dualizing the proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Lemma 4.11
.

The ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse cohomology satisfies the flasqueness axiom.

4.3
The stable Higson corona

The stable Higson corona was introduced in
[
10
]
.
A function
f\colon X\to D
from a coarse space
X
to a
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
D
is said to

\bullet

vanish at infinity if for each
\varepsilon>0
there is a relatively compact subset
K
such that
f
is bounded by
\varepsilon
outside of
K
,

\bullet

have
vanishing variation
if for every entourage
E
of
X
the function

\Var_{E}f\colon\quad X\to[0,\infty),\qquad x\mapsto\sup\{\|f(x)-f(y)\|\mid(x,y)\in E\}

vanishes at infinity.

Denote by
\overline{\mathfrak{b}}(X;D)
the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of all bounded functions of vanishing variation from
X
to
D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}
, where
{\mathfrak{K}}
denotes the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of compact operators on a separable infinite dimensional Hilbert space. Furthermore, we define
\overline{\mathfrak{b}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
to be the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of all bounded functions of vanishing variation from
X
into the stable multiplier algebra
{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)\coloneqq{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})
of
D
such that
f(x)-f(y)\in D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}
for all
x,y\in X
which lie in the same coarse component.
Both of them contain the ideal
\mathrm{B}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})
of all bounded functions
X\to D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}
vanishing at infinity. The
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
\overline{\mathfrak{b}}(X;D)
,
\overline{\mathfrak{b}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
,
\mathrm{B}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})
have been introduced in
[
10
, proof of Proposition 3.7]
with the additional condition that the functions should also be Borel, but all statements that are relevant to us still hold without this condition.

The
stable Higson corona of
X
with coefficients in
D
is the quotient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra

\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\coloneqq\overline{\mathfrak{b}}(X;D)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})

and the
reduced stable Higson corona of
X
with coefficients in
D
is the quotient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra

\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\coloneqq\overline{\mathfrak{b}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}).

All of the above function algebras are clearly contravariantly functorial under closeness classes of coarse maps.

If
X
is moreover a topological coarse space, then there are the sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras of continuous functions

\mathrm{C}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\subset\mathrm{B}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}),\qquad\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X;D)\subset\overline{\mathfrak{b}}(X;D),\qquad\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\subset\overline{\mathfrak{b}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D),

which are contravariantly functorial under continuous coarse maps and the inclusions induce isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\cong\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X;D)/\mathrm{C}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\cong\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)/\mathrm{C}_{0}(X;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}),

(4.2)

see
[
10
, proof of Proposition 3.7]
.
The right hand sides are the familiar original definitions of the stable Higson coronas
[
10
, Definitions 3.2 and 5.4]
.

For our purposes, we need the relative versions:

Definition 4.12
.

If
(X,A)
is a pair of coarse spaces, then we define its (
unreduced and reduced
)
relative stable Higson coronas with coefficients in
D
as the kernels of the restrictions to
A
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\ker(\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\to\mathfrak{c}(A;D)),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\ker\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\to\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(A;D)\big).

If
(X,A)
is even a pair of topological coarse spaces, then we also define its (
unreduced and reduced
)
relative stable Higson compactifications with coefficients in
D
as the kernels of the restrictions to
A
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\ker(\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X;D)\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(A;D)),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\ker\big(\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(A;D)\big).

Note that
\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)=\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)
and
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X,A;D)=\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)
whenever
A
meets all coarse components of
X
, whereas for
A=\varnothing
we recover
\mathfrak{c}(X;D)
,
\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
,
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X;D)
and
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
. Also,
\mathfrak{c}(X;D)=\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,K;D)
for any non-empty relatively compact
K\subset X
.
The relative stable Higson coronas are clearly contravariantly functorial under coarse maps of pairs and the relative stable Higson compactifications are so under continuous coarse maps of pairs.

Remark 4.13
.

For coarsely disconnected spaces
X
, it is debatable what the correct definition of the reduced stable Higson corona is. One could also ask for
f(x)-f(y)\in D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}
for all
x,y\in X
, not just for those in the same connected component, just like the reduced singular (co-)chain complexes are obtained from the unreduced ones by augmentation with one copy of
\mathbb{Z}
and not with one copy for each connected component of the space. As a result, the
\mathrm{K}
-theory of this reduced version of the stable Higson corona would not satisfy flasqueness (cf. Lemma
4.16
), which is in analogy to reduced singular (co-)homology not vanishing on disjoint unions of more than one contractible spaces.

Our choice of definition is due to the fact that flasqueness is an indispensable technical tool and we need a version of this coarse cohomology theory satisfying this axiom.
Later on, in Section
5.4
, we will also discuss a more flexible notion of topological reduced coarse (co-)homology, which also allows for componentwise augmentation.

Note that we would immediately obtain long exact sequences for the
\mathrm{K}
-theory groups
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D))
and
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\big)
if only the restriction maps to
A
were surjective. Unfortunately, this is a rather delicate problem which has only been solved in special cases. Willett had shown it for pairs of coarsely connected proper metric spaces for the reduced stable Higson coronas with trivial coefficients
D=\mathbb{C}

[
40
, Lemma 3.4]
, but the proof also works for arbitrary coefficients and the claim for the unreduced stable Higson corona is an easy consequence. We will show in Lemma
4.14
below how to deduce the generalization to coarsely disconnected proper metric spaces.
Bunke and Engel have discussed a generalization to a bigger class of coarse spaces, but also could not overcome all dificulties
[
5
, Section 5]
.

Lemma 4.14
.

If
X
is a proper metric space and
A\subset X
, then the restriction maps
\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\to\mathfrak{c}(A;D)
and
\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\to\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(A;D)
are surjective.

Proof.

The reduced and unreduced cases are clearly equivalent to each other, and they follow from surjectivity of the restriction map
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X;D)\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(A;D)
, so let us show the latter.

First of all we may assume that
A
meets all coarse components of
X
, because the extensions can simply be chosen to be zero on all coarse components which are disjoint from
A
.

Second, it suffices to consider the case, where
X
and
A
have at most countably many coarse components. In order to see this, let
f\in\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(A;D)
. For each
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
we find a relatively compact subset
K_{R,n}\subset A
such that
\Var_{E_{R}}f(x)<\frac{1}{n}
for all
x\in A\setminus K_{R,n}
. As each
K_{R,n}
meets only finitely many coarse components of
A
, their union
\bigcup_{R,n}K_{R,n}
are contained in a countable union of coarse components of
A
, which we denote by
A^{\prime}\subset A
, and let
X^{\prime}\subset X
be the union of the corresponding coarse components of
X
. For all
x\in A\setminus A^{\prime}
we therefore have
\Var_{E_{R}}f(x)<\frac{1}{n}
for all
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
, so that
f
is a constant function on the coarse component containing
x
.
Thus, if we know that
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X^{\prime};D)\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(A^{\prime};D)
is surjective, then we can pick any preimage
F^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X^{\prime};D)
of
f|_{A^{\prime}}
and extend it to a function
F\in\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X;D)
which is constant on each coarse component of
X\setminus X^{\prime}
and such that
F|_{A}=f
.

Thirdly, we can deduce the statement from the one for coarsely connected spaces as follows. Let
S\subset\big\{n^{2}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\big\}
be a set of square numbers which has the same cardinality as the set
\Pi_{0}(A)
of coarse components of
A
(and hence also
X
) and let
S\to A,s\mapsto x_{s}
be a map which induces a bijection
S\to\Pi_{0}(A)
. If
d\colon X\times X\to[0,\infty]
is the original coarsely disconnected proper metric, then we can define the coarsely connected proper metric
d^{\prime}\colon X\times X\to[0,\infty)
on
X
as the largest metric such that
d^{\prime}\leq d
and
d(x_{s},x_{t})\leq|s-t|
for all
s,t\in S
. It is then clear that changing the metric from
d
to
d^{\prime}
does not change the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(X;D)
and
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(A;D)
.

Finally, for coarsely connected proper metric spaces the claim is proven exactly as
[
40
, Lemma 3.4]
.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

If
X
is a proper metric space and
C\subset A\subset X
satisfy the coarse excisiveness condition, then restriction to
X\setminus C
yields isomorphism

\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)\cong\mathfrak{c}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;D)
and
\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)\cong\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;D)
.

Proof.

Injectivity is straightforward: If a function on
X
represents an element of the kernel, then its restrictions to both
A
and
X\setminus C
vanish at infinity. These two sets cover
X
and the relatively compact subsets of
X
are exactly the union of relatively compact subsets of
A
with relatively compact subsets of
X\setminus C
. This implies that the function itself vanishes at infinity, and therefore represents the zero element.

For surjectivity we use that the right square of the diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X\setminus C;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(A\setminus C;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is a pullback diagram. For coarsely connected spaces and trivial coefficients, this is
[
40
, Lemma 3.3]
, but the proof goes through for coarsely disconnected spaces and arbitrary coefficients as well. Furthermore, the same is true for the analogous unreduced version of the diagram.
The claim now follows from an easy diagram chase.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

For flasque proper metric spaces
X
, the group
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)
vanishes.

Proof.

This is the straightforward adaption of
[
40
, Proposition 3.7]
(see also
[
10
, Theorem 5.2]
) to the case of not necessarily coarsely connected spaces and with coefficients.
Let us briefly recall Willett’s sketch of proof, to see why our definition of the reduced stable Higson corona for coarsely disconnected spaces is the one fulfilling this property.

Denote by
H
the infinite dimensional separable Hilbert space on which
{\mathfrak{K}}\coloneqq{\mathfrak{K}}(H)
is modelled, such that we also have
{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)={\mathfrak{B}}(D\otimes H)
. Choose an isometric isomorphism
H\cong\oplus_{\mathbb{N}}H
and let
V_{n}\colon H\to H
be the isometric inclusion as the
n
-th summand with respect to this decomposition. Furthermore, let
\phi\colon X\to X
be the coarse map from the definition of flasqueness. For every
f\in\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
one now defines

\nu f\colon\quad X\to{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D),\qquad x\mapsto\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\id_{D}\otimes V_{n})\circ f(\phi^{n}(x))\circ(\id_{D}\otimes V_{n})^{*}.

For each two
x
,
y
in the same coarse component of
X
, the sequences
(\phi^{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\phi^{n}(y))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
stay close to each other and go to infinity. Hence the vanishing variation of
f
implies that the differences
f(\phi^{n}(x))-f(\phi^{n}(y))
converge in norm to zero for
n\to\infty
. As they are also compact, the difference

\nu f(x)-\nu f(y)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\id_{D}\otimes V_{n})\circ\big(f(\phi^{n}(x))-f(\phi^{n}(y))\big)\circ(\id_{D}\otimes V_{n})^{*}

is compact, too. Note that the part of this argument using vanishing variation does not work for
x
,
y
in different coarse components, and that is the reason why this lemma only holds for our definition of the reduced stable Higson corona and not the alternative mentioned in Remark
4.13
.

Furthermore, it is straightforward to check that
\nu f
has vanishing variation and that the resulting
*
-homomorphism
\nu\colon\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
descends to a
*
-homomorphism
\nu\colon\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\allowbreak\to\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
on the corona. The proof is then completed by the following Eilenberg swindle: one shows that on
\mathrm{K}
-theory we have
\nu_{*}(x)=x+\nu_{*}(x)
for all
x\in\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)
, so the latter group vanishes.
∎

Theorem 4.17
.

The contravariant functors
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\big)
and
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D))
are coarse cohomology theories on the category of proper metric spaces and coarse maps. The first one satisfies the flasqueness axiom and the second one the coronality axiom.

Proof.

The long exact sequences and excision are immediate from Lemmas
4.14
and
4.15
. The coronality axiom for the second one is clear, because
\mathfrak{c}(K;D)=0
for all relatively compact spaces
K
.

The flasqueness axiom for the first one follows from
4.16
for the absolute groups together with the long exact sequence for the relative groups.
Flasqueness also implies the homotopy invariance of the first functor by Lemma
3.6
.

The homotopy invariance of the second functor is a bit tricky, but it can also be deduced from the flasqueness of the first one as follows.
The proof of Lemma
3.6
worked by showing that the hypothesis of Lemma
3.5
is satisfied.
Thus, we already know that the first functor
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\big)
satisfies this hypothesis and it remains to show that the second functor
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D))
does so as well.
Given any pair
(X,A)
of proper metric spaces, we denote by
\Pi_{0}\coloneqq\Pi_{0}(X,A)
the set of those coarse components of
X
which do not contain any points of
A
.
Then there are canonical short exact sequences

0\to\mathfrak{c}(Y,B;D)\to\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(Y,B;D)\to\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}\to 0

for all those pairs
(Y,B)
of proper metric spaces derived from
(X,A)
which appear in Lemma
3.5
.
Consider the maps between the associated long exact
\mathrm{K}
-theory sequences induced by the maps
i_{0}
,
i_{\rho}
and
p
.
For
i_{0}
this yields a diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{2-p}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}\big(\overline{X}_{0}^{\rho};D\big)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{2-p}\Bigl(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}\Bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{1-p}\big(\mathfrak{c}\big(\overline{X}_{0}^{\rho};D\big)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{2-p}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{2-p}\Bigl(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}\Bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{1-p}(\mathfrak{c}(X;D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{1-p}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}\big(\overline{X}_{0}^{\rho};D\big)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{1-p}\Bigl(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}\Bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{1-p}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{1-p}\Bigl(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}\Bigr)}

and the five-lemma implies that the middle verticle map is an isomorphism. Similarly, the maps
i_{\rho}
and
p
induce isomorphisms under the second functor.
∎

We now briefly discuss two equivariant generalizations for pairs of coarsely connected proper metric
\Gamma
-spaces, where we assume that the coefficient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
D
is a
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra. In this case, there are canonical actions of
\Gamma
on
\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)
and
\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)
in which
\gamma[f]
is represented by the function
\gamma f\colon x\mapsto\gamma(f(x\gamma))
. Recall that we only consider discrete groups (which even have to be countable, because they act properly on coarsely connected proper metric spaces) and thus do not have to worry about continuity of the group actions.

Theorem 4.18
.

If
\rtimes\Gamma
denotes an exact crossed product functor in the sense of
[
2
, Definition 3.1]
, then the functors
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\rtimes\Gamma\big)
and
\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D)\rtimes\Gamma)
are
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse cohomology theories satisfying the flasqueness or the coronality axiom, respectively.

Proof.

Exactness and excision again follow directly from Lemmas
4.14
and
4.15
, and coronality of the unreduced versions is again clear.

The flasqueness axiom for the reduced version is proven exactly as in the non-equivariant case, because the
*
-homomorphism
\nu
in the proof of Lemma
4.16
will now be equivariant.

Homotopy invariance of the reduced version follows. For the unreduced version we prove it just as in the proof of the previous theorem, where the induced
\Gamma
-action on
\Pi_{0}\coloneqq\Pi_{0}(X,A)
and the action on
D
turn
\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}
into a
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra.
∎

The above equivariant version is not the most sophisticated. According to the work of Emerson and Meyer
[
11
,
12
]
, better choices are
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D))
and
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}\big(\Gamma,\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\big)
.
We do not need to recall their definiton, because the only thing relevant to us is that there are natural isomorphisms

\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(\Gamma,C)\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}((C\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathsf{P})\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma)\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}((C\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathsf{P})\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{red}}}\Gamma),

(4.3)

which hold for both the reduced and full crossed product functor and for any
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
C
(see
[
11
, Theorem 22]
, which is based on
[
26
, Theorems 5.2 and 10.2]
7
7

7

Separability of the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras is a standing assumption in the latter reference, but the isomorphisms here also hold for non-separable
C
, because we can take the direct limit over all separable sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras and exploit that all constructions in (
4.3
) are co-continuous under such.
). Here,
\mathsf{P}
denotes a certain
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra which supports the so-called Dirac morphism
\mathsf{D}\in\mathrm{KK}^{\Gamma}(\mathsf{P},\mathbb{C})
.
Exactly the same proof as for the previous theorem, just with
(-\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathsf{P})\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}
instead of
\rtimes
, shows the following.

Theorem 4.19
.

The functors
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}\big(\Gamma,\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\big)
and
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D))
are
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse cohomology theories satisfying the flasqueness or the coronality axiom, respectively.

Let us briefly discuss the relation between all of these theories. First of all, the Baum–Connes assembly map yields natural transformations

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}\big(\Gamma,\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\big)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma\big)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(-,-;D)\rtimes\Gamma\big),

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D)\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(-,-;D)\rtimes\Gamma),

which are compatible with the connecting homomorphisms.

Second, there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Pi_{0}}{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X,A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Pi_{0}}\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

with exact rows, where
\Pi_{0}\coloneqq\Pi_{0}(X,A)
again denotes the set of those coarse components of
X
which do not contain any points of
A
. The
\mathrm{K}
-theory of
\prod_{\Pi_{0}}{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)
,
\bigl(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)\bigr)\rtimes\Gamma
and
\bigl(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}({\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D))\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathsf{P}\bigr)\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma
, vanishes by an Eilenberg swindle and hence we can easily read off the following long exact sequences in
\mathrm{K}
-theory:

\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 213.66176pt\raise-31.8144pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 257.80832pt\raise-58.00381pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-42.03546pt\raise-67.50381pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{-*}\big(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-79.9899pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.72986pt\raise-67.50381pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{-*}\big(\big(\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\big)\rtimes\Gamma\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.4597pt\raise-79.9899pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 208.05757pt\raise-67.50381pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{-*}\big(\Gamma;\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 257.80832pt\raise-79.9899pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-4.94444pt\raise-81.7399pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{(}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\vdots}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 121.51526pt\raise-81.7399pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{(}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\vdots}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 252.86388pt\raise-81.7399pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{(}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\vdots}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(4.4)

The first of these three sequences decomposes for an unbounded and coarsely connected
X
and empty
A
into the short exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(D)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\to 0

(cf.
[
10
, Lemma 3.10]
), but the proof does not generalize to the equivariant case, because it involves evaluation
*
-homomorphisms at points of
X
, which are in general not equivariant.
The closest we come to an equivariant generalization is the following: If
X
contains a flasque subspace
B
which is contained in the union of the components in
\Pi_{0}(X,A)
and contains points from all components in
\Pi_{0}(X,A)
(in other words, the inclusion
B\subset X
induces a bijection between the set
\Pi_{0}(B)
of coarse components of
B
and
\Pi_{0}(X,A)
), then the above long exact sequences are mapped by restriction to the corresponding ones for
B
. But then the arrows leaving the reduced terms in the sequences for
(X,A)
factor through the corresponding terms for
B
, which vanish because of flasqueness. Thus, we get a decomposition of the three long exact sequences into short exact sequences in this case, too.

Third, as the long exact sequences (
4.4
) indicate, there is also a way of obtaining the reduced groups from the unreduced stable Higson corona with the help of a mapping cone construction. In fact, it is this construction which completely establishes the analogy to reduced cohomology theories as we will see later in Sections
5.4
and
6.3
(keeping Remark
4.13
in mind).
Recall that the mapping cone of a
*
-homomorphism
f\colon A\to B
is defined as the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra

\Cone(f)\coloneqq\{(a,\beta)\in A\times\mathrm{C}_{0}((0,1],B)\mid\beta(1)=f(a)\}

and if
f
is surjective, then the inclusion
\ker(f)\to\Cone(f),a\mapsto(a,0)
induces an isomorphism on
\mathrm{K}
-theory.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
\Pi_{0}\coloneqq\Pi_{0}(X,A)
, again, denote the set of coarse components of
X
which are disjoint from
A
.
Then there are canonical
(
up to a sign
)
natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\cong\mathrm{K}_{-*}\big(\Cone\big(\textstyle\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\big)\big),

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\rtimes\Gamma\big)\cong\mathrm{K}_{-*}\big(\Cone\big(\textstyle\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\big)\rtimes\Gamma\big),

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}\big(\Gamma,\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\cong\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{-*}\big(\Gamma,\Cone\big(\textstyle\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\big)\big)

under which the long exact sequences (
4.4
) are isomorphic to the long exact sequences induced by

0\to\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes\mathfrak{c}(X;D)\to\Cone(\textstyle\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to\mathfrak{c}(X;D))\to\textstyle\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to 0.

Proof.

In order to reuse the exact same proof for different algebras later on (Lemma
6.4
), we write
I\coloneqq\mathfrak{c}(X;D)
,
A\coloneqq\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
,
B\coloneqq\prod_{\Pi_{0}}{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)
and
J\coloneqq\prod_{\Pi_{0}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})
. The decisive properties are that
I
is an ideal in
A
,
J
is an ideal in
B
,
\mathrm{K}_{*}(B)=\mathrm{K}_{*}(B\rtimes\Gamma)=\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(\Gamma,B)=0
(because of an Eilenberg swindle argument) and there is a
*
-homomorphism
\iota\colon B\to A
which induces an isomorphism
B/J\cong A/I
. In particular,
\iota
restricts to
\iota^{\prime}\colon J\to I
.

We furthermore let
\pi\colon B\to B/J
be the quotient homomorphism and consider the diagram with exact rows

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes B/J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Cone(\iota)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Cone(\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Cone(\iota^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Both vertical arrows on the right are known to induce isomorphisms on
\mathrm{K}
-theory. They are the ones implementing the connecting homomorphism

\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(B/J)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{K}_{-*}(J)
.
Therefore, the five-lemma shows that the diagram induces isomorphisms between the long exact sequences in
\mathrm{K}
-theory associated to the rows.
This is exactly the first isomorphism of the claim.

For the other two, we apply the functors
-\rtimes\Gamma
and
(-\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathsf{P})\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma
to the whole diagram and note that all the properties mentioned above still hold, because the crossed products are exact and compatible with tensor products, hence also continuous and compatible with the cone construction.
∎

And last, there is another relation between the reduced and unreduced groups which is based upon the idea that an unreduced cohomology can be obtained by taking reduced cohomology of the space augmented by a distinct basepoint (cf.
[
43
, Section 6]
). But with our Definition
4.12
and in the light of Remark
4.13
we have to add not one distinct point but instead the whole set of coarse components
\Pi_{0}(X)
as isolated points, in some sense.
To this end, assume that there exists a relatively compact subspace
K\subset X
which contains points from all connected components of
X
. Note that this might not be the case if
\Gamma
is infinite. We define the space
X^{\to}\coloneqq X\cup_{K}K\times\mathbb{N}
equipped with the obvious metric. The subspace
K\times\mathbb{N}
is coarsely equivalent to
\Pi_{0}(X)\times\mathbb{N}
, considered as the coarsely disjoint union of copies of
\mathbb{N}
, and each of these copies should be interpreted as a newly added point at infinity. We note that
(X,K)\subset(X^{\to},K\times\mathbb{N})
is an excision,
K\times\mathbb{N}
is flasque and
\mathfrak{c}(X,K;D)=\mathfrak{c}(X;D)
. Therefore, the long exact sequence for the pair
(X^{\to},K\times\mathbb{N})
gives rise to isomorphisms

\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X;D))=\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X,K;D))\cong\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X^{\to},K\times\mathbb{N};D))\cong\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X^{\to};D)\big)

and analogously for the two equivariant versions.

4.4
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}
-theory of the Roe algebra

The Roe algebra is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
\mathrm{C}^{*}(X)
that can be constructed for every coarse space
X
(cf.
[
37
, Section 4.4]
), but it is usually only dealt with in the case of proper metric spaces. As most of the references only consider the latter special case, we shall stick to it.

Let us briefly recall the broad outlines of its construction as presented, e.g., in
[
22
, Section 6.3]
. Starting with a representation
\rho
of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
on a separable Hilbert space
H
which is non-degenerate (meaning that
\rho(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X))H
is dense in
H
), the Roe algebra
\mathrm{C}^{*}(X,\rho)
is defined as the norm closure of the
*
-subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{B}}(H)
consisting of all operators
T
satisfying the following two properties:

\bullet

T
is called
locally compact
, if
\rho(f)T,T\rho(f)
are compact operators on
H
for all
f\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
.

\bullet

T
has
finite propagation
, if there is
R\geq 0
such that
\rho(f)T\rho(g)=0
whenever the distance between the supports of
f,g\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
is at least
R
. The infimum of all
R\geq 0
with this property is called the
propagation

\Prop(T)
of
T
.

This
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra depends on the choice of
H
and
\rho
, but if the representation is big enough in a certain sense (it is
“ample”
, i.e., it is non-degenerate and
\rho^{-1}({\mathfrak{K}}(H))=\{0\}
), then its
\mathrm{K}
-theory
\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}(X,\rho))
is independent from the choices up to canonical isomorphism.
Therefore, the representation
\rho
is usually omitted from the notation with the understanding that one suitable representation has been chosen for each
X
.

Now, there are two well-known generalizations of the Roe algebra. The first is the equivariant Roe algebra in the case that a countable discrete group
\Gamma
acts properly and isometrically on the coarsely proper metric space
X
. Here one chooses in addition to the non-degenerate representation
\rho
also a representation
u
of the group
\Gamma
on
H
by unitaries such that
u(\gamma)\rho(f)u(\gamma)^{*}=\rho(\gamma\cdot f)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
f\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
. The triple
(H,\rho,u)
is called a
\Gamma
-
X
-module. Then the equivariant Roe algebra
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,\rho,u)
is the norm closure of the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of all locally compact operators of finite propagation in
{\mathfrak{B}}(H)
that are equivariant with respect to
u
. Again, the
\mathrm{K}
-theory groups
\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,\rho,u))
are independent up to canonical isomorphism from the
\Gamma
-
X
-module
(H,\rho,u)
if the latter has been chosen sufficiently large and then abbreviated by
\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X))
.
A detailed exposition about equivariant Roe algebras can be found in
[
41
]
.

The second generalization is to introduce a (possibly graded) coefficient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
D
into the Roe algebra, cf.
[
17
,
19
,
44
]
. This is done by replacing the Hilbert space
H
by a right Hilbert module
\mathfrak{H}
over
D
and the non-degenerate representation
\rho
is by adjointable operators on
\mathfrak{H}
.
The
\mathrm{K}
-theory of the resulting
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
\mathrm{C}^{*}(X,\rho,\mathfrak{H};D)
is again unique up to canonical isomorphism if
\rho
and
\mathfrak{H}
have been chosen sufficiently large and in this case
\rho
will be ommited from the notation. Here, sufficient largeness means that
\mathfrak{H}\cong H\otimes D
and
\rho=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\id_{D}
for an ample representation
\rho^{\prime}
on a separable Hilbert space
H

[
19
, Definition 4.5]
.

It is possible to unify the two generalizations to equivariant Roe algebras
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,\rho,u,\mathfrak{H};D)
with coefficients in a (graded)
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
D
.
In this case,
u
also has to be a representation by adjointable operators on
\mathfrak{H}
which is compatible with the
\Gamma
-action on
D
in the sense that
u(\gamma)(\xi d)=(u(\gamma)\xi)(\gamma d)
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{H}
,
d\in D
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Again, we obtain unambiguous
\mathrm{K}
-theory groups
\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))
if we start with sufficiently large
H,\rho,u
as in the equivariant case and then simply use their tensor product with
D
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{H}\coloneqq H\otimes D
with obvious induced representations of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
and
\Gamma
.

The proofs of the following statements can be found in the above-mentioned sources in the special cases just described, but the proofs generalize to the general case of equivariant Roe-algebras with coefficients.

If
A\subset X
is a
\Gamma
-invariant closed subspace of the proper metric
\Gamma
-space
X
, then
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
contains an ideal
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)
whose
\mathrm{K}
-theory is canonically isomorphic to the
\mathrm{K}
-theory of
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A;D)
.
It is the norm closure of all
T\in\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
which are supported in an
R
-neighborhood of
A
for some
R\geq 0
, that is, all those
T
for which
\rho(f)T=T\rho(f)=0
for all
f\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
with
\dist(\supp(f),A)\geq R
.
The quotient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)/\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)
is called the
\Gamma
-equivariant relative Roe algebra
. We have
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\varnothing\subset X;D)=0
and hence
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,\varnothing;D)=\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
. The long exact sequences

\dots\to\mathrm{K}_{p}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{p}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{p}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{p-1}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A;D))\to\cdots

are immediate.

The
\mathrm{K}
-theory of the
\Gamma
-equivariant relative Roe algebra with coefficients in
D
is functorial under
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse maps between pairs of proper metric
\Gamma
-spaces and the non-boundary maps in the long exact sequence above are special cases of this functoriality under the inclusion maps
(A,\varnothing)\to(X,\varnothing)\to(X,A)
.

For excision, we note that for an excision
(A\setminus C,X\setminus C)\to(X,A)
the left square in

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\setminus C;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X\setminus C;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X\setminus C,A\setminus C;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is simultaneously a pull-back and a push-out diagram (cf.
[
22
, p. 156]
for the non-equivariant case without coefficients, but the general case is proven in exactly the same way) and therefore the excision isomorphism already holds at the level of
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras.

Last but not least, the
\mathrm{K}
-theory of the Roe algebra vanishes on flasque spaces by an Eilenberg swindle (cf.
[
36
, Proposition 9.4]
, which is also readily generalized to the equivariant case with coefficients) and therefore is also satisfies the homotopy axiom.
Even more, the methods developed by Higson and Roe in
[
20
]
can be adapted to show the following.

Lemma 4.21
.

The
\mathrm{K}
-theory of the Roe algebra satisfies the strong homotopy axiom.

Proof.

Let
H\colon(X\times[0,1],\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{U}})\to(Y,d_{Y})
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant generalized coarse homotopy which maps
A\times[0,1]
into
B
.
In order to stay within the world of proper metric
\Gamma
-spaces, we may use the metric defined as follows instead of the coarse structure
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{U}}
.
Without loss of generality we can assume that the metric
d_{X}
on
X
is discrete and make a
\Gamma
-invariant choice of
c_{x}\geq 1
for all
x\in X
such that
|s-t|\leq\frac{1}{c_{x}}\implies(s,t)\in U_{x}
.
The largest metric
d
on
X\times[0,1]
with the property

\forall x,y\in X,s,t\in[0,1]\colon\ d((x,s),(y,t))\leq d_{X}(x,y)+\min\{c_{x},c_{y}\}\cdot|s-t|

is proper and
\Gamma
-invariant and
H
is still coarse as a map
(X\times[0,1],d)\to(Y,d_{Y})
.

Let
e_{t}\colon(X,A)\to(X,A)\times[0,1]
,
x\mapsto(x,t)
denote the canonical inclusions. It is then sufficient to prove that
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
induce the same map

(e_{0})_{*}=(e_{1})_{*}\colon\ \mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}((X,A)\times[0,1];D)),

where the right hand side is defined using the above metric
d
on
X\times[0,1]
.
In the non-equivariant, non-relative case without coefficients (
\Gamma=1
,
A=\varnothing
,
D=\mathbb{C}
) this has been proved in
[
20
, Sections 5 and 6]
, albeit with a slightly different metric on
X\times[0,1]
.
The proof relies heavily on calculations with idempotents, which is not adequate for the relative case
A\not=\varnothing
and for non-unital coefficient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
D
. Therefore, we use the same tools but apply them a bit differently to obtain the general result.

Recall the following technique to define elements of the
\mathrm{K}
-theory of a
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
C
from
[
20
, Section 4]
:
Let
{\mathcal{M}}(C)
denote its multiplier algebra and define
{\mathcal{D}}(C)\coloneqq\{(a,b)\in{\mathcal{M}}(C)\mid a-b\allowbreak\in C\}
.
Then there is a split short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{*}(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{*}({\mathcal{D}}(C))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{*}({\mathcal{M}}(C))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{*}}
\textstyle{0,}

where
j\colon c\mapsto(c,0)
,
\pi\colon(a,b)\mapsto b
and
s\colon b\mapsto(b,b)
.
An element of
\mathrm{K}_{*}(C)
can then be defined as the image of an element of
\mathrm{K}_{*}({\mathcal{D}}(C))
under
\Theta\coloneqq(j_{*})^{-1}\circ(\id-s_{*}\circ\pi_{*})
.

Furthermore, we recall that the asymptotic algebra of a
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
C
is defined as
\mathfrak{A}(C)\coloneqq\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty);C)/\mathrm{C}_{0}([1,\infty);C)
and an asymptotic morphism from another
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
B
to
C
is a
*
-homomorphism
\psi\colon B\to\mathfrak{A}(C)
. As the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
\mathrm{C}_{0}([1,\infty);C)
is contractible, such an asymptotic morphism induces a homomorphism

\psi_{*}\colon\ \mathrm{K}_{*}(B)\xrightarrow{\Psi_{*}}\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathfrak{A}(C))\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty);C))\xrightarrow{(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}}\mathrm{K}_{*}(C).

The plan is to construct an algebra homomorphism

\Psi\colon\ \mathrm{C}\big(S^{1}\big)\otimes\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D)\to\mathfrak{A}\big(\mathrm{C}[0,1]\otimes\mathrm{C}\big(S^{1}\big)\otimes{\mathcal{D}}\big(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}((X,A)\times[0,1];D))\big)\big).

(4.5)

It will not be a
*
-homomorphism (and therefore strictly speaking not an asymptotic morphism), but as algebraic and topological
\mathrm{K}
-theory agree in degree
0
we will still obtain an induced map on
\mathrm{K}_{0}
.
8
8

8

Alternatively one can use that topological
\mathrm{K}
-theory of
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras is a special case of topological
\mathrm{K}
-theory of Banach algebras
[
3
, Chapters 5 and 8]
. Since
\Psi
will clearly be a homomorphism of Banach algebras, it will induce a homomorphism on
\mathrm{K}
-theory even without the
*
-property.

Since
\mathrm{K}_{0}(\mathrm{C}(S^{1})\otimes C)\cong\mathrm{K}_{0}(C)\oplus\mathrm{K}_{1}(C)
in topological
\mathrm{K}
-theory for any
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
C
, this induced map decomposes into two maps

\Psi_{*}\colon\ \mathrm{K}_{i}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{i}(\mathrm{C}[0,1]\otimes{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}((X,A)\times[0,1];D))),\qquad i=0,1.

Now, the evaluation
*
-homomorphisms
\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{t}}\colon\mathrm{C}[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
for
t\in[0,1]
are all homotopic to each other and therefore all of the maps

\Theta\circ(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{t}})_{*}\circ\Psi_{*}\colon\ \mathrm{K}_{i}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{i}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}((X,A)\times[0,1];D))

are equal.
For the specific
\Psi
which we are about to define and
t=0,1
, these maps are exactly
(e_{0})_{*}
and
(e_{1})_{*}
, respectively. The claim follows.

In order to carry this out, assume that
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
has been constructed on the Hilbert module
\mathfrak{H}\coloneqq H\otimes D
for a sufficiently large
\Gamma
-
X
-module
(H,\rho,u)
. Furthermore, let
\rho^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{C}[0,1]\to{\mathfrak{B}}\big(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\big)
be the representation, where
\phi\in\mathrm{C}[0,1]
acts on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by multiplication with

\tilde{\phi}\colon\ s\mapsto\begin{cases}\phi(0),&s\leq 0,\\
\phi(t),&0\leq t\leq 1,\\
\phi(1),&t\geq 1.\end{cases}

Then we choose two copies of the
\Gamma
-
(X\times[0,1])
-module
\big(H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime},u\otimes 1\big)
to construct
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X\times[0,1];D)
, that is, this
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra consists of operators on
\big(H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\otimes D\big)^{2}
.

The relevant outcome of
[
20
, Sections 6]
can be summarized as follows. There exists a bounded and equicontinuous family of maps
\big\{F_{p}\colon[0,1]\to{\mathfrak{B}}\big(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\big)\big\}_{p\in(0,1]}
such that

\bullet

the operators
F_{p}(s)
are Fredholm of index one and
F_{p}(s)^{*}
is an inverse to
F_{p}(s)
modulo compact operators,

\bullet

for fixed
s
and varying
p
, the operators
F_{p}(s)
are compact perturbations of one another,

\bullet

the propagation of
F_{p}(s)
and
F_{p}(s)^{*}
with respect to the representation
\rho^{\prime}
is bounded by
p
,

\bullet

F_{p}(0)
is independent of
p
, has zero propagation and decomposes as
F_{p}(0)=W_{0}\oplus\id_{L^{2}(0,\infty)}
, where
W_{0}
is a coisometry on
L^{2}(-\infty,0)
(i.e.,
W_{0}W_{0}^{*}=\id_{L^{2}(-\infty,0)}
) of index one,

\bullet

and similarily
F_{p}(1)
is independent of
p
, has zero propagation and decomposes as
F_{p}(1)=W_{1}\oplus\id_{L^{2}(-\infty,1)}
for an index one coisometry
W_{1}
on
L^{2}(1,\infty)
.

We can now adapt the constructions of
[
20
, Sections 5]
to our needs. Proofs of our claims can be found in that reference.
Given
\varepsilon>0
we can find a partition of unity
\{\sigma_{\varepsilon,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
on
X
such that

\bullet

each
\sigma_{\varepsilon,i}
is continuous,
\Gamma
-invariant and
\Gamma
-cocompactly supported,

\bullet

\sigma_{\varepsilon,i}\sigma_{\varepsilon,j}=0
if
|i-j|\geq 2
,

\bullet

each
\sigma_{\varepsilon,i}
is
\varepsilon 2^{-i}
-Lipschitz.

It can be constructed, for example, by starting with an analogous but non-equivariant partition of unity
\{s_{\varepsilon,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
on
[0,\infty)
and defining
\sigma_{\varepsilon,i}(x)\coloneqq s_{\varepsilon,i}(\dist(x,\Gamma\cdot x_{0}))
for some fixed
x_{0}\in X
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
one can now find
\varepsilon_{n}>0
and
p_{n,i}\in(0,1]
such that the
\Gamma
-equivariant operators

G_{n}(s)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho(\sigma_{\varepsilon_{n},i})\otimes F_{p_{n,i}}(s)\in{\mathfrak{B}}\big(H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\big)

have propagation at most
\frac{1}{n}
for all
s\in[0,1]
.
Note that these operators are all bounded by
2\sup_{p\in(0,1]}\|F_{p}\|
, because the second assumption on the partition of
unity implies

\displaystyle\|G(v)\|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl\|\sum_{i\text{ even}}(\rho(\sigma_{\varepsilon_{n},i})\otimes F_{p_{n,i}}(s))v\biggr\|+\biggl\|\sum_{i\text{ odd}}(\rho(\sigma_{\varepsilon_{n},i})\otimes F_{p_{n,i}}(s))v\biggr\|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\sum_{i\text{ even}}\|\id_{H}\otimes F_{p_{n,i}}\|^{2}\cdot\|(\rho(\sigma_{\varepsilon_{n},i})\otimes\id_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})})v\|^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad+\biggl(\sum_{i\text{ odd}}\|\id_{H}\otimes F_{p_{n,i}}\|^{2}\cdot\|(\rho(\sigma_{\varepsilon_{n},i})\otimes\id_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})})v\|^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{p\in(0,1]}\|F_{p}\|\cdot\|v\|

for all
v\in H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Furthermore, these operators are equicontinuous in
s
because of the equicontinuity of the
F_{p}
.
By the affine linear interpolation
G_{t}\coloneqq(t-n)G_{n+1}+(n+1-t)G_{n}
for
t\in[n,n+1]
we extend these maps to a bounded continuous map

G\colon\ [1,\infty)\to C[0,1]\otimes{\mathfrak{B}}\big(H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\big),\qquad t\mapsto G_{t}

and define the bounded continuous map

\displaystyle R\colon\ [1,\infty)

\displaystyle\to C[0,1]\otimes{\mathfrak{B}}\big(\big(H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\big)^{2}\big),

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto R_{t}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}2G_{t}-G_{t}G_{t}^{*}G_{t}&G_{t}G_{t}^{*}-\id_{H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\\
\id_{H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-G_{t}^{*}G_{t}&G_{t}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\!.

Each
R_{t}
is invertible with inverse

R_{t}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}G_{t}^{*}&\id_{H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-G_{t}^{*}G_{t}\\[2.15277pt]
G_{t}G_{t}^{*}-\id_{H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}&2G_{t}-G_{t}G_{t}^{*}G_{t}\end{pmatrix}

and we define the idempotents
Q\coloneqq\id_{H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\oplus 0
and
P_{t}\coloneqq R_{t}QR_{t}^{-1}
in
C[0,1]\otimes{\mathfrak{B}}\big(\big(H\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\big)^{2}\big)
.

The proof of
[
20
, Lemma 5.11]
now shows the following properties of these idempotents:

\bullet

The operators
Q\otimes\id_{D}
,
P_{t}(s)\otimes\id_{D}
for
s\in[0,1],t\in[1,\infty)
and
T\otimes\id_{(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))^{2}}
for
T\in\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}^{*}(X;D))
are all equivariant and have finite propagation. Thus, they are multipliers of
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}^{*}(X\times[0,1];D))
.

\bullet

For all
s\in[0,1],t\in[1,\infty)
and
T\in\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}^{*}(X;D))
we have

((Q-P_{t}(s))\otimes\id_{D})\circ(T\otimes\id_{(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))^{2}})\in\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}^{*}(X\times[0,1];D)).

The above-mentioned proof goes directly through for
t\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
. For all other
t
we additonally need to exploit the second of the above-mentioned properties of the
F_{p}
, i.e., that for fixed
s
and varying
p
the
F_{p}(s)
are compact perturbations of one another.

\bullet

For all
e\in\mathrm{C}\big(S^{1}\big)\otimes\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}^{*}(X;D))
the commutators

\big[P_{t}\otimes\id_{\mathrm{C}(S^{1})\otimes D},e\otimes\id_{(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))^{2}}\big]

converge in norm to zero as
t\to\infty
.

These properties imply that algebra homomorphism

\Psi\colon\ \mathrm{C}\big(S^{1}\big)\otimes\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\to\mathfrak{A}\big(\mathrm{C}[0,1]\otimes\mathrm{C}\big(S^{1}\big)\otimes{\mathcal{D}}\big(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X\times[0,1];D)\big)\big),

which is the non-relative version of (
4.5
),
can now be defined by mapping
e\in\mathrm{C}\big(S^{1}\big)\otimes\allowbreak\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}^{*}(X;D))
to the class represented by the function

t\mapsto\Biggl(s\mapsto\underbrace{((P_{t}(s)\otimes\id_{D})\circ(e(s)\otimes\id_{(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))^{2}}),(e(s)\otimes\id_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})})\oplus 0)}_{\in{\mathcal{D}}\big(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X\times[0,1];D))\big)}\Biggr).

It is not a
*
-homomorphism, because the
R_{t}
are not unitaries and hence the
P_{t}
are not self-adjoint.

To finish the proof of the absolute case, it remains to analyze what happens at
s=0
and
s=1
.
At
s=0
we have
G_{t}(0)=\id_{H}\otimes F_{p}(0)=(\id_{H}\otimes W_{0})\oplus\id_{H\otimes L^{2}(0,\infty)}
for all
t\in[1,\infty)
,
p\in(0,1]
and a calculation yields

\displaystyle P_{t}(0)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\id_{H\otimes L^{2}(-\infty,0)}&0\\
0&\id_{H}\otimes(\id_{L^{2}(-\infty,0)}-W_{0}^{*}W_{0})\end{pmatrix}\oplus\id_{(H\otimes L^{2}(0,\infty))^{2}}

\displaystyle=Q\oplus(\id_{H\otimes D}\otimes p_{0})

for a rank one orthogonal projection
p_{0}\in{\mathfrak{B}}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
which is supported over
0\in[0,1]
with respect to
\rho^{\prime}
. It is now straightforward to verify that
\Theta\circ(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{0}})_{*}\circ\Psi_{*}
is the same map as the one induced by the
*
-homomorphism
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D)\to\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}((X,A)\times[0,1];D)
,
T\mapsto T\otimes p_{0}
, and this is exactly
(e_{0})_{*}
. Analogously we obtain
\Theta\circ(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}\circ\Psi_{*}=(e_{1})_{*}
.

Since all
P_{t}(s)
have propagation bounded by one, the constructions in the last two paragraphs are compatible with taking the quotients by the ideals
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)
,
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\times[0,1]\subset X\times[0,1];D)
and then the relative case follows immediately.
∎

We summarize:

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
\Gamma
be a discrete group.
The
\mathrm{K}
-theory groups of the
\Gamma
-equivariant relative Roe algebras with coefficients in a possibly graded
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra constitute a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse homology theory on the admissible category of pairs of proper metric isometric
\Gamma
-spaces which satisfies the flasqueness and the strong homotopy axiom.

The Roe algebra can also be modified as follows such that the resulting coarse homology theory satisfies the coronality instead of the flasqueness axiom.
The compact operators in
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)
form an ideal
{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)\coloneqq\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)\cap{\mathfrak{K}}(\mathfrak{H})
which is
0
if
A=\varnothing
and equal to the
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
{\mathfrak{K}}(\mathfrak{H})^{\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
-equivariant compact operators on
\mathfrak{H}
if
A\not=\varnothing
and
X
is coarsely connected, but
{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)\subsetneqq{\mathfrak{K}}(\mathfrak{H})^{\Gamma}
if
X
is coarsely disconnected. We also define
{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\coloneqq{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(X\subset X;D)=\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\cap{\mathfrak{K}}(\mathfrak{H})\subset\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
.
We can now define the
coronal relative
\Gamma
-equivariant Roe algebras with coefficients in
D
as

\mathrm{C}^{*}_{/{\mathfrak{K}};\Gamma}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)/{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(X;D)}{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)/{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)}\cong\frac{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)}{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)+{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(X;D)}

and we note that
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{/{\mathfrak{K}};\Gamma}(X,\varnothing;D)=\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)/{\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
.
These
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras vanish if
X
is relatively compact, because in this case
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)={\mathfrak{K}}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
.
Note also that we have
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{/{\mathfrak{K}};\Gamma}(X,A;D)\cong\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D)
if
A
contains points from all coarse components of
X
.

Theorem 4.23
.

The functor
\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{/{\mathfrak{K}};\Gamma}(-,-;D))
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse homology theory on the admissible category of pairs of proper metric isometric
\Gamma
-spaces satisfying the coronality and the strong homotopy axiom.

Proof.

Functoriality and independence of
F(-,-)\coloneqq\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{/{\mathfrak{K}};\Gamma}(-,-;D))
from the chosen representation follows just as for
G(-,-)\coloneqq\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(-,-;D))
, because the constructions on the level of
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras pass to the quotients.
We know that the excision isomorphism for
G
holds already at the level of
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras and from here one readily deduces that the same is true for
F
.
The long exact sequence and coronality are clear from the construction.
Finally, strong homotopy invariance can be proven just like Lemma
4.21
simply by taking another quotient by the ideal compact operators in the last part of the proof.
∎

5
From topological to coarse (co-)homology theories

Let
\Gamma
be a fixed discrete group and let
\mathsf{CGBG}^{2}_{\Gamma}
be the category of pairs of countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces (see Definition
2.21
) of bornologically bounded geometry (see Definition
2.4
) and
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse maps. It is an admissible category in the sense of Definition
3.1
.

The purpose of this section is to construct coarse
(co-)
homology theories on
\mathsf{CGBG}^{2}_{\Gamma}
from generalized
(co-)
homology theories on so-called
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces by means of a Rips-complex construction. Coarse
(co-)
homology theories constructed in this way always satisfy the strong homotopy axiom.
For a very useful special type of
(co-)
homology theories, the so-called single-space
(co-)
homology theories which also satisfy the so-called strong excision axiom, the resulting coarse
(co-)
homology theories also satisfy the flasqueness axiom and can be related to the
(co-)
homology theories of certain coronas via the so-called transgression maps.

The idea of constructing coarse
(co-)
homology theories in this way goes back to the definition of coarse
\mathrm{K}
-theory in
[
10
]
and was generalized to non-equivariant single-space cohomology theories in
[
13
, Section 4]
. New in our present discussion are the group actions and that we take a much deeper look at the details.

A different approach to constructing coarse homology theories is via anti-Čech systems, see
[
21
, Section 2]
,
[
27
, Definition 3.5]
and

[
37
, Section 5.5]
. Unfortunately, it seems to be unsuitable for cohomology theories, because there instead of simply taking the direct limits one has to find groups satisfying
\varinjlim^{1}
-sequences, cf.
[
35
, Chapter 3]
.

5.1
Categories of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
-spaces

In this subsection we also allow arbitrary locally compact groups
\Gamma
, but we will return to discrete ones in the following two subsections.

Definition 5.1

(cf.
[
10
, Section 2]
,
[
43
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a locally compact group.
A
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space

\mathcal{X}
is an increasing sequence
X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset X_{2}\subset X_{3}\subset\cdots
of locally compact Hausdorff spaces equipped with continuous
\Gamma
-actions such that for all
m\leq n
the space
X_{m}
is closed in
X_{n}
and carries the subspace topology and the restricted
\Gamma
-action.
We call
\mathcal{X}
a
\sigma
-compact
\Gamma
-space
, if in addition all
X_{n}
are compact.
9
9

9

Note that this is not the same as what is usually known as
\sigma
-compact spaces in the literature.

By an abuse of notation we will use the symbol
\mathcal{X}
for the set
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
endowed with the induced action and the final topology, i.e., a subset
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}
is open/closed if and only if every intersection
A_{n}:=\mathcal{A}\cap X_{n}
is open/closed.
The final topology has the property that a map from
\mathcal{X}
into another topological space is continuous if and only if it is continuous on every
X_{n}
.

Note also that the final topology on
\mathcal{X}
is Hausdorff itself: If
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
are two distinct points, say
x,y\in X_{n}
, then there is a function
f\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
with
f(x)=0
and
f(y)=1
. This function can be extended inductively to all
X_{m}
with
m\geq n
, because the one-point compactification
X_{m}^{+}
is a closed subspace of the normal Hausdorff space
X_{m+1}^{+}
. Thus, one obtains a continuous extension
F\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}
with
F(x)=0
and
F(y)=1
.

Any locally compact Hausdorff
\Gamma
-space
X
can be considered also as a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space by assigning to it the constant sequence
X_{n}\coloneqq X
.

Definition 5.2
.

We say that a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
is a
subspace
of
\mathcal{Y}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{n}
if
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{Y}
is
\Gamma
-invariant,
\mathcal{X}\cap Y_{n}=X_{n}
and each
X_{n}
carries the subspace topology of
Y_{n}
and the restricted
\Gamma
-action.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
be
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces given by the filtrations
(X_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, respectively, and let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a map.

\bullet

We call
f
a
\sigma
-map
if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f(X_{m})\subset Y_{n}
.
Note that such an
f
is continuous in the final topologies if and only if all the restricted maps
f|_{X_{m}}\colon X_{m}\to Y_{n}
are continuous.

\bullet

We call a continuous
\sigma
-map
f

proper
if the preimages of all
\sigma
-compact subspaces of
\mathcal{Y}
under
f
are
\sigma
-compact subspaces of
\mathcal{X}
, or equivalently, if the restricted maps
f|_{X_{m}}
:
X_{m}\to Y_{n}
are proper continuous maps.

Using these notions we can introduce the categories of spaces on which we will consider
(co-)
homology theories.

Definition 5.4
.

\bullet

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
be the category whose objects are pairs
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
of
\sigma
-compact
\Gamma
-spaces with
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}
a closed subspace
10
10

10

Recall that subspaces are always
\Gamma
-invariant by Definition
5.2
.
and whose morphisms between
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})
are continuous
\Gamma
-equivariant
\sigma
-maps
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
which restrict to maps
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
.

\bullet

Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
be the category whose objects are pairs
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
of
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces with
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}
a closed subspace and whose morphisms between
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})
are proper continuous
\Gamma
-equivariant
\sigma
-maps
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
which restrict to maps
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
.

\bullet

The category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
has objects the
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces and its morphisms between
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are proper continuous
\Gamma
-equivariant
\sigma
-maps
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}
, where
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{X}
is an open subspace. The composition of two such morphisms
\mathcal{X}\supset\mathcal{U}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Y}\supset\mathcal{V}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{Z}
is defined to be the morphism
\mathcal{X}\supset f^{-1}(\mathcal{V})\xrightarrow{g\circ f|_{f^{-1}(\mathcal{V})}}\mathcal{Z}
.

\bullet

The category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}
is a mixture of the last two: The objects
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
are the same as in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
and the morphisms are of the form
\mathcal{X}\supset\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Y}
as in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
but with the additional property that they map
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{U}
into
\mathcal{B}
. If this is the case, then the restriction
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{B}
is also morphisms in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
.

\bullet

The categories
\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}
and
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
are the restrictions of the above categories to pairs of compact Hausdorff spaces and locally compact Hausdorff spaces, respectively. Equivalently, one can define them by removing all
\sigma
’s in the above four parts of the definition. Everything that will be said in the remainder of this section about the above four categories is also true in complete analogy for these non-
\sigma
-counterparts and therefore we shall not mention the corresponding statements explicitly.

\bullet

If the index
\Gamma
is omitted from the notation, this means we do not consider group actions, i.e.,
\Gamma=1
.

The definition of the morphisms in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
may appear a bit strange at first sight, but note that they are exactly in one-to-one correspondence with the basepoint-preserving continuous
\Gamma
-equivariant
\sigma
-maps between the
one-point
\sigma
-compactifications

\mathcal{X}^{+}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}^{+}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}\cup\{\infty\},

i.e., the
\sigma
-compact spaces defined by the corresponding sequence of one-point compactifications.
In other words, the functor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
which maps a space
\mathcal{X}
to the pair of spaces
(\mathcal{X}^{+},\{\infty\})
and a morphism
