\mathcal{X}\supset\mathcal{U}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{Y}
to the continuous
\sigma
-map

f^{+}\colon\quad(\mathcal{X}^{+},\infty)\to(\mathcal{Y}^{+},\infty),\qquad x\mapsto\begin{cases}f(x),&x\in\mathcal{U},\\
\infty,&x\in\mathcal{X}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{U}\end{cases}

is a full and faithful functor.

Conversely, a right inverse to the above functor is the composition of the full and faithful inclusion of categories

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}

with the faithful inclusion of categories

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}

and the functor

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}

which maps pairs of spaces
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
to their difference
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A}
and morphisms
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})
which are given by the data
\mathcal{X}\supset\mathcal{U}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{Y}
to the morphism
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A}\supset f^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{B})\xrightarrow{f|_{f^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{B})}}\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{B}
.

Remark 5.5
.

The most convenient way to construct a morphism
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}
in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
is by providing a morphism
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})
with
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{V}
in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
and applying the above construction, i.e., applying the last two of those three functors.

Now that we have explained our categories of spaces, we can go on to product spaces and homotopies.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space and
\mathcal{Y}
a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma^{\prime}
-spaces given by the filtrations
(X_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, respectively. Their
product

\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
is the
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}
-space defined by the sequence of locally compact Hausdorff
\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}
-spaces
(X_{n}\times Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Again, this is not a cartesian product in the category of
\sigma
-locally compact Hausdorff spaces.

Note that the union
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}\times Y_{n}
equipped with the final topology is homeomorphic to the product of the spaces
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
and
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{n}
and hence the interpretation of the expression
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
as a topological space is unambiguous.

The following two lemmas and the corollary are obvious. The first lemma says that the cross product is functorial in both variables, the second treats inclusions of one of the factors as slices and projections onto one of the factors, and the corollary explains adequate notions of homotopy.

Lemma 5.7
.

The assignment

\big((\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}),\,(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})\big)\mapsto(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{Y}\cup\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{B})

gives rise to functors

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}\times\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{2},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}\times\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{2},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}\times\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{+,2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{+,2}

and the assignment
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})\mapsto\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
gives rise to a functor

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}\times\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{+}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{+}.

In each case, the morphisms are mapped in the obvious way.
They are compatible with each other in the sense that they commute with the three functors mentioned in the paragraph before Remark
5.5
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fixing
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})

(
or only
\mathcal{Y})
in the above lemma gives rise to functors

\displaystyle-\times(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})\colon\ \boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{2},

\displaystyle-\times(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})\colon\ \boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{2},

\displaystyle-\times(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})\colon\ \boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{+,2},

\displaystyle-\times\mathcal{Y}\colon\qquad\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma\times\Gamma^{\prime}}^{+}

and for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
the maps
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\times\{y\}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
are proper continuous
\sigma
-maps and constitute a natural transformation from the respective identity functors to each of the above functors.

Conversely, if
\mathcal{Y}
is
\sigma
-compact and
\mathcal{B}=\varnothing
, then the projection maps
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
are proper continuous
\sigma
-maps and constitute a natural transformation from
-\times\mathcal{Y}
to the identity functors in each of these categories and this natural transformation is left inverse to the abovementioned ones.

Corollary 5.9
.

For
\mathcal{Y}=I\coloneqq[0,1]
the unit interval with trivial
\Gamma^{\prime}=1
-action and
\mathcal{B}=\varnothing
, the above natural tranformations give rise to an adequate notion of homotopy in each of the four categories
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
.

5.2
Generalized (co-)homology theories and the Rips complex

From all of the categories of spaces that we have introduced in the last section,
\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
are almost admissible categories in the sense of Eilenberg and Steenrod
[
9
, Section I.1]
, except for the slight difference that the objects are not just topological spaces but contain a little bit of additional information in the form of the group action and possibly the filtration by closed subspaces.

Definition 5.10
.

By a
(co-)
homology theory (or
\Gamma
-equivariant
(
co-
)
homology theory
) on
\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
or
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
we understand a collection of
covariant (contravariant) functors from this category to the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded abelian groups together with natural transformations, the (co-)boundary maps, just as in the axiomatic set-up by Eilenberg and Steenrod,
which satisfies the homotopy, exactness and excision axioms, but not necessarily the dimension or the additivity axioms, cf.
[
9
, Sections I.3 and I.3c]
.

In the following, we will always be interested in such a homology theory
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}
or such a cohomology theory
\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}
on the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
. The purpose of this section is to show that applying them to the Rips complex yields coarse
(co-)
homology theories on
\mathsf{CGBG}^{2}_{\Gamma}
which satisfy the strong homotopy axiom. From now on,
\Gamma
is a discrete group.

We first construct the Rips complex for locally finite countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces. If
E
is a
\Gamma
-invariant entourage, then we define
P_{E}(X)
to be the geometric realization of the simplicial complex with vertex set
X
and with
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in X
spanning an
n
-simplex iff
(x_{i},x_{j})\in E
for all
0\leq i,j\leq n
.
Note that we could equally well consider only symmetric entourages for this construction, because
P_{E}(X)=P_{E\cap E^{-1}}(X)
, but there is no need at all for doing so.
The space
P_{E}(X)
is locally compact, because
X
is locally finite, and it is a proper
\Gamma
-space, because
E
is
\Gamma
-invariant and the action of
\Gamma
on
X
is proper.

Let
S\coloneqq\{E_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
be a countable generating set of the coarse structure. We may assume
{\Delta_{X}}=E_{0}\subset E_{1}\subset\cdots
and that each entourage
E
is contained in one of the
E_{n}
, because otherwise we simply replace
E_{n}
by the finite union of all the entourages that can be obtained from
E_{1},\dots,E_{n}
by up to
n
operations of the form (3)–(5) of Definition
2.1
. Furthermore, we may assume that all
E_{n}
are
\Gamma
-equivariant, because the
\Gamma
-action is isocoarse.

Definition 5.11
.

The
Rips complex
of
X
with respect to the generating set
S
is the
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\mathcal{P}(X,S)\coloneqq\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}P_{m}(X)
, where we have abbreviated
P_{m}(X)\coloneqq P_{E_{m}}(X)
.

Note that as a topological space, the Rips complex is simply the full simplicial complex of
X
, but as a
\sigma
-space it depends on the choice of the generating set
S
, because the sequence of the
P_{m}(X)
is an essential part of the data.
Nevertheless, the following lemma justifies to drop the generating set from the notation and simply write
\mathcal{P}(X)
.

Lemma 5.12
.

For two different choices of generating sets
S
,
S^{\prime}
as above, the resulting Rips complexes are canonically isomorphic in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}
of
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces and proper continuous
\Gamma
-equivariant maps via the identity map
\id\colon\mathcal{P}(X,S)\to\mathcal{P}(X,S^{\prime})
.

Proof.

If
S^{\prime}
is given by the sequence
{\Delta_{X}}=E^{\prime}_{0}\subset E^{\prime}_{1}\subset\cdots
, then for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
E_{m}\subset E^{\prime}_{n}
and hence
P_{E_{m}}(X)\subset P^{\prime}_{E_{n}}(X)
. Therefore the identity is a
\sigma
-map.
∎

Given in addition a
\Gamma
-invariant subspace
A\subset X
, its Rips complex
\mathcal{P}(A)
with respect to the restricted generating set
\{E\cap(A\times A)\mid E\in S^{\prime}\}
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{P}(X)
.
If
f\colon(X,A)\to(Y,B)
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse map between two pairs of locally finite countably generated coarse
\Gamma
-spaces, then affine linear extension defines a proper continuous
\Gamma
-invariant
\sigma
-map
\mathcal{P}(f)\colon(\mathcal{P}(X),\mathcal{P}(A))\to(\mathcal{P}(Y),\mathcal{P}(B))
, i.e., a morphism in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
, the subset
P_{E}(X)
being mapped to
P_{F}(Y)
if
(f\times f)(E)\subset F
.
It is readily verified that close maps
X\to Y
induce homotopic maps
\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
, where the homotopy is affine linear, but we even have the following much stronger result.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
f,g\colon(X,A)\to(Y,B)
be
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse maps between two pairs of locally finite countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces. If
f
,
g
are
\Gamma
-equivariantly generalized coarsely homotopic, then their induced maps between the Rips complexes
\mathcal{P}(f)
,
\mathcal{P}(g)
:
(\mathcal{P}(X),\mathcal{P}(A))\to(\mathcal{P}(Y),\mathcal{P}(B))
are
\Gamma
-equivariantly homotopic.

Proof.

We recall the choices that we have already made at the beginning of the proof of Lemma
4.4
.
Let
H\colon(X\times[a,b],\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{U}})\to(Y,\mathcal{E}_{Y})
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant generalized coarse homotopy between
f
and
g
which maps
A\times[a,b]
into
B
.
For each point
x\in X
we choose
a=s_{x,0}<s_{x,1}<\dots<s_{x,k_{x}}=b
such that
(s,s_{x,j})\in U_{x}
and
(s,s_{x,j+1})\in U_{x}
for all
s\in[s_{x,j},s_{x,j+1}]
,
j=0,\dots,k_{x}-1
and we can assume that
s_{x,j}=s_{x\gamma,j}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
x\in X
and
j=0,\dots,k_{x}-1=k_{x\gamma}-1
.
Now, if
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}
span an
n
-simplex in
P_{E}(X)
for some entourage
E\in\mathcal{E}_{X}
and if
s\in\bigcap_{i=0}^{n}[s_{x_{i},j_{i}},s_{x_{i},j_{i}+1}]
, then the points

(x_{0},s_{x_{0},j_{0}}),\quad\dots,\quad(x_{n},s_{x_{n},j_{n}}),\quad(x_{0},s_{x_{0},j_{0}+1}),\quad\dots,\quad(x_{n},s_{x_{n},j_{n}+1})

span an
2n
-simplex in
P_{(E\mathbin{\bar{\times}}{\Delta_{[a,b]}})\circ E_{\mathcal{U}}}(X)
.
Due to this property the map
\tilde{H}\colon\mathcal{P}(X)\times[a,b]\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
we are now about to define will obviously be a
\sigma
-map.

We start the construction of
\tilde{H}
by demanding that
\tilde{H}
shall agree with
H
on the subset
\{(x,s_{x,j})\mid x\in X\wedge j=0,\dots,k_{x}\}
. Then we extend it affine linearly to a map on
X\times[a,b]
, that is

\tilde{H}(x,(1-r)s_{x,j}+rs_{x,j+1})\coloneqq(1-r)\cdot H(x,s_{x,j})+r\cdot H(x,s_{x,j+1})

for all
j=0,\dots,k_{x}-1
and
r\in[0,1]
.
And finally, we extend it again affine linearly to all of
\mathcal{P}(X)\times[a,b]
by

\tilde{H}\bigg(\sum_{x\in X}\lambda_{x}x,s\biggr)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in X}\lambda_{x}\tilde{H}(x,s).

Continuity of
\tilde{H}
is obvious and topological properness of
\tilde{H}
follows directly from the coarse
geometric
properness of
H
.
It is also a
\sigma
-map, because it clearly maps
P_{E}(X)
into
P_{(H\times H)((E\mathbin{\bar{\times}}{\Delta_{[a,b]}})\circ E_{\mathcal{U}})}(Y)
, and it obviously maps
\mathcal{P}(A)
into
\mathcal{P}(B)
.
Finally,
\Gamma
-equivariance follows from the
\Gamma
-invariant choice of the
s_{x,j}
.
∎

We are now going to apply these constructions to discretizations of pairs
(X,A)
of countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces of bornologically bounded geometry, i.e., objects in
\mathsf{CGBG}^{2}_{\Gamma}
. According to Lemma
2.26
there are
\Gamma
-invariant discretizations
\iota_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\subset X
and
A^{\prime}\subset A
such that there are
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse equivalences
\pi_{X}\colon X\to X^{\prime}
and
\pi_{A}\colon A\to A^{\prime}
which are the identities on
X^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
, respectively.
By performing the obvious modifications of
X^{\prime}
and
\pi_{X}
, if necessary, we may assume that
A^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
and
\pi_{A}=\pi_{X}|_{A}
. Then the inclusion
\iota_{X}\colon(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})\to(X,A)
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse equivalence with
\pi_{X}
being a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse inverse up to closeness.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{*}^{\Gamma}
be a generalized
\Gamma
-equivariant homology theory or
\mathrm{E}^{*}_{\Gamma}
a generalized
\Gamma
-equivariant cohomology theory. Then its
coarsification

\mathrm{EX}_{*}^{\Gamma}
or
\mathrm{EX}^{*}_{\Gamma}
is defined by the groups

\mathrm{EX}_{*}^{\Gamma}(X,A)\coloneqq\mathrm{E}_{*}^{\Gamma}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}))\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathrm{EX}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A)\coloneqq\mathrm{E}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})),

respectively, where
(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
denotes some fixed discretization of
(X,A)
of the type described above.

Theorem 5.15
.

Up to canonical isomorphism, the coarsifications
\mathrm{EX}_{*}^{\Gamma}(X,A)
and
\mathrm{EX}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A)
are independent of the choice of discretization. Furthermore, they are the groups of a
\Gamma
-equivariant
(
co-
)
homology theory that satisfies the strong homotopy axiom.

Proof.

If there is another pair of spaces with discretization
(Y^{\prime},B^{\prime})\xleftrightharpoons[\iota_{Y}]{\pi_{Y}}(Y,B)
of the same type and if
f\colon(X,A)\to(Y,B)
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse map, then the
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse map
f^{\prime}\coloneqq\pi_{Y}\circ f\circ\iota_{X}\colon(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})\to(Y^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is up to closeness independent of the choice of
\pi_{Y}
and consequently the induced map on the Rips complexes
\mathcal{P}(f)\colon(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}))\to(\mathcal{P}(Y^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(B^{\prime}))
is up to homotopy in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
independent of the choice of
\pi_{Y}
by Lemma
5.13
. Thus, we obtain an induced group homomorphism
f_{*}\colon\mathrm{EX}_{*}^{\Gamma}(X,A)\to\mathrm{EX}_{*}^{\Gamma}(Y,B)
and
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{EX}^{*}_{\Gamma}(Y,B)\to\mathrm{EX}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A)
which is independent of the choice of
\pi_{Y}
.

Also, if
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
are generalized coarsely homotopic, then so are
f_{1}^{\prime}
,
f_{2}^{\prime}
and therefore
\mathcal{P}(f_{1})
,
\mathcal{P}(f_{2})
are homotopic in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
, so
(f_{1})_{*}=(f_{2})_{*}
and
(f_{1})^{*}=(f_{2})^{*}
.

If there is a third pair of spaces with discretization
(Z^{\prime},C^{\prime})\xleftrightharpoons[\iota_{Z}]{\pi_{Z}}(Z,C)
and
g\colon(Y,B)\to(Z,C)
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse map, then
g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}
is close to
(g\circ f)^{\prime}
and hence
\mathcal{P}(g^{\prime})\circ\mathcal{P}(f^{\prime})
is homotopic to
\mathcal{P}(g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime})
, so
g_{*}\circ f_{*}=(g\circ f)_{*}
and
f^{*}\circ g^{*}=(g\circ f)^{*}
.

Applying all of this to the identity map between the same pair of spaces
(X,A)
but equipped with different discretizations
(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
, we see that the groups
\mathrm{EX}_{*}^{\Gamma}(X,A)
and
\mathrm{EX}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A)
are independent of the choice of discretization up to canonical isomorphism.

Thus, so far we have shown that the groups form a covariant or a contravariant functor from
\mathsf{CGBG}_{\Gamma}^{2}
to the category of abelian groups which satisfies the strong homotopy axiom. The exactness axiom for
\mathrm{EX}_{*}^{\Gamma}
,
\mathrm{EX}^{*}_{\Gamma}
follows immediately from the exactness axiom for
\mathrm{E}_{*}^{\Gamma}
,
\mathrm{E}^{*}_{\Gamma}
.

Finally, but most complicatedly, it remains to show the excision axiom.
Let
(X\setminus C,A\setminus C)\subset(X,A)
be an excision (cf. Definition
3.2
). We can choose discretizations
C^{\prime}\subset C
,
A^{\prime}\subset A
and
X^{\prime}\subset X
as above such that
C^{\prime}\subset A^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
and then
(X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime},A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})\subset(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is an excision, too.
Let
\alpha\colon\mathcal{P}(X)\to[0,1]
be the continuous
\Gamma
-invariant function which is
1
on
A^{\prime}
,
0
on
X^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime}
and then extended over all simplices affine linearily. In the same way we construct
\beta\colon\mathcal{P}(X)\to[0,1]
being
1
on
C^{\prime}
and
0
on
X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime}
. We define the closed subspace
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\alpha^{-1}\bigl[\frac{1}{3},1\bigr]
and the open subspace
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\beta^{-1}\bigl(\frac{2}{3},1\bigr]
of
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
.
Note that the closure of
\mathcal{C}
is contained in the interior of
\mathcal{A}
, because
\beta\leq\alpha
, and therefore the inclusions
(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C})\to(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{A})
are topological excisions and thus induce isomorphisms on
(co-)
homology.

The proof can now be finished by showing that the subspaces
\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C}
are deformation retracts, because then homotopy invariance and the long exact sequences will show that the inclusions
(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}))\to(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime}))\to(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C})
induce isomorphisms on
(co-)
homology.

Constructing a retraction
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})
is straightforward: A point
x\in\mathcal{A}
has a decomposition
x=\lambda a+(1-\lambda)b
with
a\in\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})
and
b\in\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime})
in which
\lambda\geq\frac{1}{3}
. In particular,
\lambda\not=0
and therefore
a
is uniquely determined. Due to this uniqueness, mapping
x\mapsto a
gives us a continuous
\Gamma
-equivariant retraction which is homotopic to the identity via the obvious affine linear homotopy. Furthermore, both the retraction and the homotopy respect the filtration, because if
x
lies in a simplex, then
a
and the whole path between
x
and
a
lie in the same simplex. Exactly the same procedure provides us with a deformation retraction
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
.

The tricky part is to construct the deformation retraction
\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
and here we will need the excisiveness condition. Let
{\Delta_{X^{\prime}}}=E_{0}\subset E_{1}\subset\cdots
be a sequence of symmetric
\Gamma
-invariant entourages of
X^{\prime}
such that every entourage is contained in some
E_{n}
, and set
E_{-1}=\varnothing
.
We choose a point
x_{O}\in O
in each
\Gamma
-orbit
O
of
X^{\prime}
,
O=x_{O}\Gamma
, and let
\Gamma_{x_{O}}
denote the stabilizer of
x_{O}
. Now, there is a unique
n_{O}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x_{O}\in\Pen_{E_{n}}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})\setminus\Pen_{E_{n-1}}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
and therefore in particular
O=x_{O}\Gamma\subset\Pen_{E_{n}}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
.
Choose
y_{O}\in A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime}
such that
(x_{O},y_{O})\in E_{n}
. Note that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{x_{O}}
we have
(x_{O}\gamma,y_{O}\gamma^{\prime}\gamma)\in E_{n}
by
\Gamma
-invariance of
E_{n}
. Also, for
O\subset X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime},x_{O}\in X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime}
, we have
y_{O}=x_{O}
.

We can now define a map
r\colon\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\to\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
as the affine linear extension of the
\Gamma
-equivariant extension of the mapping
x_{O}\mapsto\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{x_{O}}}y_{O}\gamma^{\prime}
, that is

r\colon\ \sum_{O\subset X^{\prime},\,\gamma\in\Gamma}\lambda_{O,\gamma}x_{O}\gamma\mapsto\sum_{O\subset X^{\prime},\,\gamma\in\Gamma,\,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{x_{O}}}\lambda_{O,\gamma}y_{O}\gamma^{\prime}\gamma.

This map is clearly well defined,
\Gamma
-equivariant, continuous and restricts to the identity on
\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
.

It is not a
\sigma
-map itself, but its restriction to
\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C}
is: If
x\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C}
, then each simplex of
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
which contains
x
must have at least one vertex in
A^{\prime}

(
because
\alpha(x)\geq\frac{1}{3}
)
and one vertex in
X^{\prime}\setminus C

(
because
\beta(x)\leq\frac{2}{3}
)
. Thus, if this particular simplex lies in
P_{E}(X^{\prime})
, then all of its vertices lie in
\Pen_{E}(A^{\prime})\cup\Pen_{E}(X^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})\subset\Pen_{E_{n}}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
only depends on
E
. This shows
(\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C})\cap P_{E}(X^{\prime})\subset P_{E}(\Pen_{E_{n}}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime}))
and
r
clearly maps this subset into
P_{E_{n}\circ E\circ E_{n}}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})
.

The same applies to the homotopy between
r
and the identity on
\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C}
given by affine linear interpolation. Therefore,
\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}\setminus C^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{C}
is a deformation retract.
∎

5.3
Single space (co-)homology theories and transgression maps

An important class of coarse
(co-)
homology theories are the coarsifications of so-called single-space
(co-)
homology theories. We will show in this section that they always satisfy the flasqueness axiom and admit the so-called transgression maps.

Definition 5.16
.

A
(co-)
homology theory on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
or
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
is called a
single-space
(
co-
)
homology theory
(or
\Gamma
-equivariant single space
(
co-
)
homology theory
, if we want to emphasize the group action), if it factors through
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
up to natural isomorphism. This property is called the
strong excision axiom
.
Equivalently, these
(co-)
homology theories can be axiomatized using solely the absolute
(co-)
homology groups
11
11

11

Therefore the name.
as follows:

\bullet

A
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space homology theory
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}
for
\sigma
-locally compact spaces
is a collection of covariant homotopy functors
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{n}
indexed by
n\in\mathbb{Z}
from the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
to the category of abelian groups together with
boundary maps

\partial_{n}\colon\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{n-1}(\mathcal{A})
such that the long sequences

\dots\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\partial_{n}}\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{n-1}(\mathcal{A})\to\cdots

(5.1)

are exact and natural in
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})\in\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}
.

\bullet

Dually, a
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space cohomology theory
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}
for
\sigma
-locally compact spaces
is a collection of contravariant homotopy functors
\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{n}
indexed by
n\in\mathbb{Z}
from the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
to the category of abelian groups together with
coboundary maps

\delta^{n}\colon\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{n}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{n+1}(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A})
such that the long sequences

\dots\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{n}(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{n}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{n}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\delta_{n}}\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{n+1}(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A})\to\cdots

(5.2)

are exact and natural in
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})\in\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+,2}
.

In the same way,
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space
(co-)
homology theories can be introduced on
\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
,
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
and
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
.

This definition has already been well-established for a long time on the categories
\mathsf{CH}^{2}
,
\mathsf{LCH}^{2}
,
\mathsf{LCH}^{+}
. In
[
13
, Definition 4.13]
we gave
\sigma
-versions for
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}^{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}^{+}
under the name “generalized Steenrod
(co-)
homology theories”, where “Steenrod” was meant to express that the strong excision axiom holds. However, we drop this naming in the present paper to avoid confusion, because other authors use it as a qualifier for the cluster axiom, which says that homology turns disjoint unions of spaces into products of homology groups and co-homology turns disjoint unions into direct sums (cf.
[
41
, Definition B.2.2]
).
We will not use the cluster axiom here, but it should be noted that it might be very useful for the calculation of
(co-)
homology of the Rips complexes by cellular methods.

For the remainder of this section,
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}
will always denote a
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space homology theory and
\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}
will always denote a generalized
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space cohomology theory for
\sigma
-locally compact spaces and their coarsifications will be denoted by
\mathrm{EX}^{\Gamma}_{*}
and
\mathrm{EX}_{\Gamma}^{*}
, respectively, as before.

Lemma 5.17
.

Coarsifications of single-space
(
co-
)
homology theories satisfy the flasqueness axiom.

Proof.

Let
X
be a flasque space, witnessed by
\phi\colon X\to X
, and let
\iota\colon X^{\prime}\subset X
be a discretization as in Lemma
2.26
with
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse equivalence
\pi\colon X\to X^{\prime}
. Then we define the map
(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}))^{+}\times[0,\infty]\to(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}))^{+}
as being equal to
\mathcal{P}(\pi\circ\phi^{n}\circ\iota_{X})
on
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\times\{n\}
, interpolating affine linearily inbetween and mapping everything else (i.e.,
\{\infty\}\times[0,\infty]\cup\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\cup\{\infty\}
) to
\infty
. From the assumptions on
\phi
, it is straightforward to see that this is a
\Gamma
-equivariant proper continous
\sigma
-map. Furthermore, it is readily checked that it descends to a homotopy in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
which shows that
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
is homotopy equivalent to the empty set. The long exact sequence shows that
(co-)
homology vanishes on the empty set, and therefore its coarsification vanishes on
X
.
∎

Given a coarse space
X
, we let
\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)
denote the commutative
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of all bounded functions
X\to\mathbb{C}
of vanishing variation and
\mathrm{B}_{0}(X)\coloneqq\mathrm{B}_{0}(X;\mathbb{C})
the ideal of all functions vanishing at infinity. The
Higson corona

\partial_{h}X
is by definition the maximal ideal space of the quotient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X)
, i.e., the compact Hausdorff space with
\mathrm{C}(\partial_{h}X)\cong\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X)
.
Note that
\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)
and
\mathrm{B}_{0}(X)
are clearly contravariantly functorial and hence
\partial_{h}X
is covariantly functorial under closeness classes of coarse maps.
In particular, coarsely equivalent spaces have homeomorphic Higson coronas.
We call a compact Hausdorff space
\partial X
a
Higson dominated corona
for the coarse space
X
if there is a continuous surjective map
\partial_{h}X\twoheadrightarrow\partial X
.

If
X
is moreover a topological coarse space, then
\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)
contains the sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of continuous functions
\mathrm{C}_{h}(X)=\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)\cap\mathrm{C}(X)
which contains
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
. Its maximal ideal space is a compactification
\overline{X}^{h}
of
X
, called the
Higson compactification
. We have
\mathrm{C}(\partial_{h}X)\cong\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X)\cong\mathrm{C}_{h}(X)/\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)\cong\mathrm{C}\big(\overline{X}^{h}\setminus X\big)
, where the proof of the second isomorphism works exactly as the proof of (
4.2
), see
[
10
, proof of Proposition 3.7]
.
Thus, the Higson corona is the boundary of this compactification. A compactification
\overline{X}
of
X
with boundary
\partial X
is called
Higson dominated
, if there is a continuous surjection
\overline{X}^{h}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{X}
which restricts to the identity on
X
, or equivalently, if the closure of each entourage
E\subset X\times X
intersects
\overline{X}\times\partial X\cup\partial X\times\overline{X}
only within
\partial X\times\partial X
.
In this case
\partial X\coloneqq\overline{X}\setminus X
is a Higson dominated corona.

If
X
comes equipped with a coarse
\Gamma
-action, then
\partial_{h}X
and
\overline{X}^{h}
are
\Gamma
-spaces. In this case we will always assume that Higson dominated coronas and compactifications
\partial X,\overline{X}
are also
\Gamma
-spaces and that the surjections are
\Gamma
-equivariant.

Now assume that
X
is a countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-space of bornologically bounded geometry and
\partial X
a Higson dominated corona. Let
X^{\prime}\subset X
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant discretization as in Lemma
2.26
.
For each
\Gamma
-invariant entourage
E
of
X^{\prime}
, the Rips complex
P_{E}(X^{\prime})
becomes a topological isocoarse
\Gamma
-space if we additionally equip it with the canonical coarse structure characterized by the properties that the inclusion
X^{\prime}\subset P_{E}(X^{\prime})
is a coarse equivalence and the simplices are uniformly bounded.
Then there is a Higson dominated compactification of
P_{E}(X^{\prime})
with the same corona
\partial X
, which can be constructed as follows: Note that the corona
\partial X
corresponds to a unital sub-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of
\mathrm{C}(\partial_{h}X)\cong\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X)\cong\mathrm{B}_{h}(X^{\prime})/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{B}_{h}(P_{E}(X^{\prime}))/\mathrm{B}_{0}(P_{E}(X^{\prime}))\cong\mathrm{C}_{h}(P_{E}(X^{\prime}))/\mathrm{C}_{0}(P_{E}(X^{\prime}))
, that is, it is of the form
C/\mathrm{C}_{0}(P_{E}(X^{\prime}))
for a unital sub-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
C\subset\mathrm{C}_{h}(P_{E}(X^{\prime}))
and the maximal ideal space of
C
is a Higson-dominated compactification
\overline{P_{E}(X^{\prime})}
of
P_{E}(X^{\prime})
with boundary
\partial X
. Applying it to the sequence of Rips complexes
P_{E_{0}}(X^{\prime})\subset P_{E_{1}}(X^{\prime})\subset\cdots
we obtain a sequence of compact Hausdorff
\Gamma
-spaces
\overline{P_{E_{0}}(X^{\prime})}\subset\overline{P_{E_{1}}(X^{\prime})}\subset\cdots
, where the inclusions are the identity on
\partial X
. Thus, this sequence is a
\sigma
-compactification
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
of
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
with boundary
\partial X
.

Assume furthermore that
A\subset X
is a coarse
\Gamma
-invariant subspace. Then the image of
\mathrm{C}(\partial X)
under the restriction map
\mathrm{C}(\partial_{h}X)\cong\mathrm{B}_{h}(X)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(X)\to\mathrm{B}_{h}(A)/\mathrm{B}_{0}(A)\cong\mathrm{C}(\partial_{h}A)
corresponds to a Higson dominated corona
\partial A
of
A
. By construction we have a surjection
\mathrm{C}(\partial X)\to\mathrm{C}(\partial A)
, so
\partial A
can be considered a subspace of
\partial X
.
If
X^{\prime}\subset X
and
A^{\prime}\subset A
are two discretizations as in Lemma
2.26
with
A^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
, then we obtain corresponding compactifications
\overline{P_{E}(A^{\prime})}
with boundary
\partial A
for each entourage
E
of
X
. The function algebras
\mathrm{C}(\overline{P_{E}(A^{\prime})})
are exactly the images of
\mathrm{C}(\overline{P_{E}(X^{\prime})})
under the restriction maps
\mathrm{C}_{h}(P_{E}(X^{\prime}))\to\mathrm{C}_{h}(P_{E}(A^{\prime}))
, so we can consider
\overline{P_{E}(A^{\prime})}
as a subspace of
\overline{P_{E}(X^{\prime})}
. Thus, the
\sigma
-compactification
\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})}
is also a subspace of
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
and we note that
\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})}\cap\partial X=\partial A
.

In the proof of Theorem
5.15
we have seen that different choices of discretizations
(X^{\prime\prime},A^{\prime\prime})
lead to homotopy equivalences
(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}))\simeq(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime\prime}))
in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
which are canonical up to homotopy. They clearly extend to homotopy equivalences
(\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})},\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})})\simeq(\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime\prime})},\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime\prime})})
in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
which are the identity on the boundaries
(\partial X,\partial A)
and are also canonical up to homotopy. Therefore, the next definition is independent of the choice of discretizations.

Definition 5.18
.

Let
(X,A)
be a pair of countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces of bornologically bounded geometry,
\partial X
a Higson dominated corona and
\partial A
the corresponding corona of
A
.
The
transgression maps
are the connecting homomorphisms

\mathrm{EX}_{*+1}^{\Gamma}(X,A)\to\mathrm{E}_{*}^{\Gamma}(\partial X,\partial A)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{E}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\partial X,\partial A)\to\mathrm{EX}^{*+1}_{\Gamma}(X,A)

associated to the pair of
\sigma
-locally compact spaces
\big(\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})},\partial X\setminus\partial A\big)
.

Important for applications (see, e.g., Theorems
6.6
and
6.12
below) is the question when transgression maps are isomorphisms. The long exact sequences and homotopy invariance immediately imply the following result.

Lemma 5.19
.

If
\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})}
is a deformation retract of
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
in the category
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{CH}_{\Gamma}
, then the transgression maps are isomorphisms.

5.4
Reduced (co-)homology and reduced transgression

In the absolute case
A=\varnothing
, there is a similar result to Lemma
5.19
if we consider a reduced version of the transgression maps.
To define them, we first introduce reduced (co-)homology for
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces
\mathcal{X}
equipped with a decomposition
\mathcal{X}=\coprod_{i\in\pi}\mathcal{K}_{i}
as a coproduct in the category of
\sigma
-locally compact spaces (indexed by an arbitrary set
\pi
) with the following properties: First, we assume that each
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is
\sigma
-compact. And second, the subsets
\mathcal{K}_{i}
do not have to be closed under the
\Gamma
-action, but we do assume that each group element maps each
K_{i}
homeomorphically onto some
K_{j}
.
In case of the trivial decomposition into a single
\sigma
-compact subset
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{X}
and without group action we will recover the usual definition of reduced (co-)homology.

For each nonempty
\sigma
-compact space
\mathcal{K}
, let
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\mathcal{K}\times[0,1]/\mathcal{K}\times\{1\}
be the closed cone and
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\mathcal{K}\times(0,1]/\mathcal{K}\times\{1\}
the open cone over
\mathcal{K}
.
Furthermore we define
\mathcal{C}\varnothing
and
\mathcal{O}\varnothing
to be the one-point space.
Thanks to
\sigma
-compactness of
\mathcal{K}
, the closed cone is again
\sigma
-compact and the open cone is
\sigma
-locally compact.
Then we define the closed and open multicones of
\mathcal{X}
as
\mathcal{C}^{+}\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\coprod_{i\in\pi}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\coprod_{i\in\pi}\mathcal{O}\mathcal{K}_{i}
, respectively. They are canonically
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces.

Definition 5.20
.

The
reduced

(co-)
homology groups of a decomposed
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\mathcal{X}
as above are defined as

\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X})\coloneqq\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}(\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\coloneqq\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*+1}(\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X}).

To understand this definition, we furthermore define the suspension of a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\mathcal{X}
as
\Sigma\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}\times(0,1)
.
The obvious deformation retraction of
\mathcal{X}\times[0,1]
to
\mathcal{X}\times\{1\}
induces a homotopy equivalence in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
between
\mathcal{X}\times[0,1)
and the empty space and hence all single space
(co-)
homology theories vanish on them.

Definition 5.21
.

The
suspension isomorphisms
are the connecting homomorphisms

\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}(\Sigma\mathcal{X})\cong\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\cong\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*+1}(\Sigma\mathcal{X})

in the long exact sequences associated to the pairs of spaces
(\mathcal{X}\times[0,1),\mathcal{X}\times\{0\})
.

Now, we can canonically consider
\pi
itself as a discrete
\Gamma
-space. As such, it is embedded in the multicones
\mathcal{C}^{+}\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X}
as the subspace of apices. As
\mathcal{C}^{+}\mathcal{X}
is contractible onto
\pi
and by using the suspension isomorphisms, the long exact sequences associated to the pair
(\mathcal{C}^{+}\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}\times\{0\})
become

\displaystyle\cdots\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}(\pi)\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\pi)\to\cdots,

\displaystyle\cdots\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\pi)\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*+1}(\pi)\to\cdots.

(5.3)

These sequences imply in particular that reduced
(co-)
homology vanishes if the canonical
\Gamma
-equivariant continuous
\sigma
-map
\mathcal{X}\to\pi
is a homotopy equivalence.

The connection to the well-known definition of reduced (co-)homology is now as follows:
If
\mathcal{X}
contains a
\Gamma
-invariant subset which meets each
\mathcal{K}_{i}
in exactly one point, then the map
\mathcal{X}\to\pi
has a right inverse in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}
and the exact sequences yield canonical isomorphisms

\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X})\cong\ker(\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\pi))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\cong\operatorname{coker}(\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\pi)\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X})).

In the non-equivariant case and for the trivial decomposition, such that
\pi
itself is a one-point space, these isomorphisms are exactly the classical definitions of the reduced groups via augmentation with a point.
Analogously, for non-trivial decompositions, we recognize the idea that an augmentation with more than one point has been performed.
However, we emphasize that such a right inverse
\pi\to\mathcal{X}
need not exist in the equivariant case, so our definiton via cones is superior.

Given a closed subspace
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{X}

equipped with the decomposition into the subsets
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{K}_{i}
, we have
\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{A}=\Sigma(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{A})
.
As
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{K}_{i}
might be empty even if
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is not, we point out that it is important to allow empty sets in the decomposition and to define the cones over the empty space to be the one-point space for this to work.
Then, the long exact sequences associated to the pairs
(\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{A})
become

\displaystyle\cdots\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{A})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\mathcal{A})\to\cdots,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\cdots\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}_{\Gamma}^{*-1}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{E}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{A})\to\cdots.

(5.5)

Lemma 5.22
.

Up to signs, there is a canonical natural transformations from the reduced homological long exact sequence (
5.4
) to the corresponding unreduced one (
5.1
) and dually from the unreduced cohomological long exact sequence (
5.2
) to the corresponding reduced one (
5.5
).

Proof.

The transformations are defined by applying naturality under the morphism
(\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{A})\allowbreak\supset(\Sigma\mathcal{X},\Sigma\mathcal{A})
and the suspension isomorphisms. The only nontrivial claim is that the boundary maps
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}(\Sigma\mathcal{X},\Sigma\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\Sigma\mathcal{A})
and
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\mathcal{A})
correspond to each other under suspension (and dually for cohomology), but this is only true up to the sign
-1
. Its proof is a fairly standard argument from algebraic topology, although concrete references seem to be hard to find: First, one rewrites the boundary map using suspension as

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X}\times\{1\}\cup A\times[0,1],\mathcal{A}\times\{0\})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(A\times(0,1],\mathcal{A}\times\{0\})\cong\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\Sigma A)\cong\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\mathcal{A})

and similarily for the boundary map for
(\Sigma\mathcal{X},\Sigma\mathcal{A})
.
The claim is then equivalent to the fact that the two ways of performing the double suspension
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}\big(\Sigma^{2}A\big)\cong\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\Sigma A)\cong\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\mathcal{A})
(suspending the first copy of
(0,1)
first and then the second copy versus the other way around) coincide up to the sign
-1
, where the sign arises from the reflection of the square
(0,1)^{2}
along the diagonal.
How the proof works can be seen in the follow-up article
[
45
, Lemma 4.6.3]
, where a more complicated analogous statement is shown for suspension in coarse (co-)homology.
∎

Now, let
X
be a countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-space of bornologically bounded geometry and
X^{\prime}\subset X
a discretization.
For each coarse component
K
of
X
, we furthermore consider a Higson-dominated corona
\partial K
in a way compatible with the
\Gamma
-action. More precisely, we assume that their coproduct
\partial X\coloneqq\coprod_{K\in\Pi_{0}(X)}\partial K
is a locally compact
\Gamma
-space and moreover a quotient
\Gamma
-space of
\coprod_{K\in\Pi_{0}(X)}\partial_{h}K
. We can attach each
\partial K
to
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\cap K)
and obtain the
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}\coloneqq\coprod_{K\in\Pi_{0}(X)}\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\cap K)}
, which contains
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
as an open and
\partial X
as a closed subspace.
We consider all of them as equipped with the decomposition indexed by
\Pi_{0}(X)
.
12
12

12

Note however, that the notation is slightly misleading: Here, the spaces
\partial X
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
are not (
\sigma
-)compact, if
\Pi_{0}(X)
is not finite.

Definition 5.23
.

We call a locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\partial X\coloneqq\coprod_{K\in\Pi_{0}(X)}\partial K
as above together with its decomposition a
componentwise Higson dominated corona
of
X
.
The associated
reduced transgression maps
are the connecting homomorphisms

\mathrm{EX}_{*+1}^{\Gamma}(X)\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}_{*}^{\Gamma}(\partial X)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{\mathrm{E}}^{*}_{\Gamma}(\partial X)\to\mathrm{EX}^{*+1}_{\Gamma}(X)

in the long exact sequences of reduced (co-)homology associated to the pair
(\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})},\partial X)
.

We now obtain the following reduced analog of Lemma
5.19
.

Lemma 5.24
.

If each
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\cap K)}
is contractible and all the contractions together are compatible with the
\Gamma
-action, then the reduced transgression maps are isomorphisms.

Proof.

The hypothesis means nothing else than that the
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\coprod_{K\in\Pi_{0}(X)}\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}\cap K)}
is homotopy equivalent to
\Pi_{0}(X)
, canonically considered as a discrete
\Gamma
-space. But its reduced (co-)homology groups vanish, because
\mathcal{O}^{+}\Pi_{0}(X)=(0,1]\times\Pi_{0}(X)
is homotopy equivalent to the empty set. The claim now follows from the exact sequences (
5.4
) and (
5.5
).
∎

As an example, the central Theorem 5.7 of
[
13
]
says that the hypothesis is satisfied in the non-equivariant case for the combing compactification of a proper metric space equipped with an expanding and coherent combing.

Let us close this section by mentioning the following relation between reduced and unreduced co-assembly.
13
13

13

Compare to the end of Section
4.3
.
If
X
is a countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-space of bornologically bounded geometry and
K\subset X
is a subspace whose intersection with each coarse component of
X
is bounded, then we define the countably generated isocoarse
\Gamma
-space
X^{\to}\coloneqq X\cup_{K}K\times\mathbb{N}
of bornologically bounded geometry by equipping it with the obvious coarse structure. Then reduced transgression for
X^{\to}
canonically identifies with relative transgression of the pair
(X^{\to},K\times\mathbb{N})
and with unreduced transgression for
X
.

6
Further examples

In the following subsections we take a closer look at coarsifications of some specific directly constructed
(co-)
homology theories on
\sigma
-spaces and partly also their transgression maps.

6.1
Singular (co-)homology

Locally finite singular homology
\mathrm{HS}^{\mathrm{lf}}_{*}(-,-;M)
and compactly supported singular cohomology
\mathrm{HS}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(-,-;M)
with coefficients in an abelian group
M
are known to be
(co-)
homology theories on
\mathsf{LCH}^{2}
. Let
\mathrm{CS}^{\mathrm{lf}}_{*}(-,-,M)
and
\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(-,-,M)
denote their respective
(co-)
chain complexes. If
\Gamma
is in addition a discrete group which acts on
M
from the left, then we can perform the following modifications to the
(co-)
chain complexes to obtain
(co-)
homology theories on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
.
First we pass to the subcomplexes
\mathrm{CS}^{\mathrm{lf},\Gamma}_{*}(-,-;M)\subset\mathrm{CS}^{\mathrm{lf}}_{*}(-,-;M)
of
\Gamma
-equivariant chains and the quotient complex
\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{c},\Gamma}^{*}(-,-;M)\coloneqq\Gamma\backslash\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(-,-;M)
, just as we did in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, to obtain functors on
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
. And second, if
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
is a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space with closed subspace
\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_{n}
, then we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}^{\mathrm{lf},\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A};M)\coloneqq\varinjlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{CS}^{\mathrm{lf},\Gamma}_{*}(X_{n},A_{n};M),

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{c},\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A};M)\coloneqq\varprojlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{c},\Gamma}^{*}(X_{n},A_{n};M),

where all the maps in the directed systems are injective or surjective, respectively.
The usual proofs go through to show that these
(co-)
chain complexes define
(co-)
homology theories
\mathrm{HS}^{\mathrm{lf}}_{*}(-,-;M)
and
\mathrm{HS}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(-,-;M)
on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{2}
and for the sake of computations we note that we have
\mathrm{HS}^{\mathrm{lf},\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A};M)\cong\varinjlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{HS}^{\mathrm{lf},\Gamma}_{*}(X_{n},A_{n};M)
and a Milnor-
{\varprojlim}^{1}
-sequence

0\to{\varprojlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}^{1}\mathrm{HS}_{\mathrm{c},\Gamma}^{*-1}(X_{n},A_{n};M)\to\mathrm{HS}_{\mathrm{c},\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A};M)\to\varprojlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{HS}_{\mathrm{c},\Gamma}^{*}(X_{n},A_{n};M)\to 0.

Now, if
(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is a pair of locally finite countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces, then its ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse
(co-)
chain complex is nothing else but the
\Gamma
-equivariant locally finite/compactly supported simplicial
(co-)
chain complex of the pair of
\sigma
-simplicial complexes
(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}))
(defined in the completely analogous way). The usual proofs using barycentric subdivision can be adapted to the present
\sigma
-situation with
\Gamma
-actions to show that the singular and simplicial
(co-)
homology of
(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime}))
coincide. This shows
\mathrm{HX}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X^{\prime},A^{\prime};M)\cong\mathrm{HS}^{\mathrm{lf},\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});M)
and
\mathrm{HX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X^{\prime},A^{\prime};M)\cong\mathrm{HS}_{\mathrm{c},\Gamma}^{*}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});M)
, that is, the ordinary
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse
(co-)
homology is the coarsification of
\Gamma
-equivariant locally finite/compactly supported singular
(co-)
homology.

6.2
Alexander–Spanier (co-)homology

Unfortunately, singular
(co-)
homology does not satisfy strong excision, so they do not provide transgression maps. Instead one has to use other
(co-)
homology theories like Alexander–Spanier cohomology and its dual.

Alexander–Spanier cohomology is known to be a single-space cohomology theory on
\mathsf{LCH}^{+,2}
(see in particular
[
38
, Section 6.6]
or
[
24
, Section 1.7]
for a proof of the strong excision property).
By taking inverse limits of Alexander–Spanier cochain complexes one can easily generalize this cohomology theory to a single-space cohomology theory on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}^{+,2}
whose coarsification is the (non-equivariant) ordinary coarse cohomology. As a consequence we obtain transgression maps from the Alexander–Spanier cohomology of Higson-dominated coronas to the coarse cohomology of a space, and one can indeed show that they agree with Roe’s original transgression map from
[
35
, Section 5.3]
.
All of this was done in
[
13
, Section 4.4]
.

Care has to be taken if one tries to dualize Alexander–Spanier cohomology (or Čech cohomology) in order to obtain a homology theory which satisfies the strong excision axiom, because most attempts of dualization destroy the long exact sequences. Massey pointed out how to do it correctly
[
24
,
25
]
, providing us with a single-space homology theory on
\mathsf{LCH}^{+,2}
, which we call Alexander–Spanier homology. It can be generalized easily to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}^{+,2}
by taking direct limits.
If
(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is a pair of uniformly locally finite countably generated coarse spaces, then all the pairs of Rips complexes
(P_{E}(X^{\prime}),P_{E}(A^{\prime}))
are finite dimensional and the Alexander–Spanier homology of these pairs are isomorphic to their locally finite cellular homology by
[
24
, Section 4.9(4)]
and hence also to their locally finite simplicial homology. Consequently, for spaces of coarsely bounded geometry the coarsification of Alexander–Spanier homology is (non-equivariant) ordinary coarse homology and we obtain transgression maps from ordinary coarse homology to the Alexander–Spanier homology of Higson-dominated coronas.

The proofs of the basic properties of Alexander–Spanier
(co-)
homology are somewhat involved, and therefore some non-trivial effort would probably have to be invested to see if
\Gamma
-actions can be implemented into the definitions in the naive ways seen in Sections
4.1
and
6.2
. We are not going to pursue this question any further.

6.3
Coarse
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}
-theory and coassembly

Given a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
and a
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
D
, the associated function
*
-algebra

\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{X};D)\coloneqq\{f\colon\mathcal{X}\to D\mid\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon f|_{X_{n}}\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(X_{n};D)\}

equipped with the countable family of
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-seminorms
\|f\|_{n}\coloneqq\|f|_{X_{n}}\|
and the pull-back action
\gamma\cdot f\colon x\mapsto\gamma\cdot(f(x\gamma))
is a so-called
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra, i.e., it is a complex topological
*
-algebra whose topology is Hausdorff, generated by a countable family of
\Gamma
-equivariant
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-seminorms and is complete with respect to this family of seminorms. Equivalently, it is the inverse limit in the category of topological complex
*
-algebras of an inverse system of
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras indexed over the natural numbers (cf.
[
29
]
).

Referring to a previous remark, the
\Gamma
-action on a
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra is isometric with respect to all the seminorms. Instead one could also pose the weaker condition that the
\Gamma
-action is only continuous with respect to the topology on the whole algebra. The latter results in what we would call
\Gamma
-
\sigma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras, i.e., the
\sigma
and
\Gamma
are interchanged to account for the different order in which they are implemented into the definition.

Via pullback of functions, we obtain a contravariant functor
\mathrm{C}_{0}(-;D)\colon\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}

into the category of
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras and
\Gamma
-equivariant
*
-homomorphisms, the latter being automatically continuous. In the case
D=\mathbb{C}
this functor is actually an equivalence between the categories
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+})^{\mathrm{op}}
and the category of commutative
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
\mathsf{c}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}
, i.e., a Gelfand–Naimark type duality.

If
-\rtimes\Gamma
is any crossed product functor on the category of
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras, then we obtain a corresponding crossed product functor from
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}
to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{C}^{*}
by applying it to an associated inverse system of
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras and taking the inverse limit afterwards. It is a direct consequence of
[
29
, Proposition 5.3(2)]
that the resulting crossed product functor is exact, if we started with an exact crossed product functor.
We can now extend
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}
to
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras by turning (
4.3
) into a definition, as was done in
[
11
,
12
]
.

Definition 6.1
.

Using Phillips’
\mathrm{K}
-theory for
\sigma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
[
30
,
31
]
,
for any exact crossed product functor and any
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
C
we define

\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(\Gamma,C)\coloneqq\mathrm{K}_{*}((C\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathsf{P})\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma).

Furthermore, the
\Gamma
-equivariant
\mathrm{K}
-theory with coefficients in a
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
D
is defined as

\mathrm{K}_{\Gamma}^{*}(-;D)\coloneqq\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{-*}(\Gamma,\mathrm{C}_{0}(-;D)).

Lemma 6.2
.

The functors
\mathrm{K}_{\Gamma}^{*}(-,D)
and
\mathrm{K}_{-*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(-;D)\rtimes\Gamma)
for any exact crossed product functor
-\rtimes\Gamma
are
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space cohomology theories on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
and they all agree on proper
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces.

Proof.

The first statement follows directly from the long exact sequences and homotopy invariance of Phillips’
\mathrm{K}
-theory together with continuity and exactness of the maximal tensor product and the crossed product functors in consideration.

If
X
is a proper locally compact
\Gamma
-space, then
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X;D)
is a proper
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra and
[
8
, Remark 3.4.16]
implies that all crossed products
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X;D)\rtimes\Gamma
, even the non-exact ones, coincide. Thus, the same is true for
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{X};D)\rtimes\Gamma
if
\mathcal{X}
is a proper
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space.

As observed in
[
11
, Section 2.7]
, the Baum–Connes assembly maps yield isomorphisms
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{-*}(\Gamma,C)=\mathrm{K}_{-*}((C\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathsf{P})\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma)\cong\mathrm{K}_{-*}(C\mathbin{{\rtimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\Gamma)
for all proper
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
C
. Applying this to
C=\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{X};D)
for proper
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces
\mathcal{X}
finishes the proof.
∎

Definition 6.3
.

By the second part of the preceding lemma, the coarsifications of
\mathrm{K}_{\Gamma}^{*}(-,D)
and
\mathrm{K}_{-*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(-;D)\rtimes\Gamma)
for all exact crossed product functors
-\rtimes\Gamma
agree and we call it the
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse
\mathrm{K}
-theory

\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(-,D)
.

We are also interested in the reduced
\mathrm{K}
-theory groups of
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces
\mathcal{X}
equipped with a decomposition
\mathcal{X}=\coprod_{i\in\pi}\mathcal{K}_{i}
as in Section
5.4
. In
[
12
, Section 2.3]
, Emerson and Meyer gave a definition thereof for undecomposed
\sigma
-compact
\Gamma
-spaces
\mathcal{K}
as the
\mathrm{K}
-theory of a certain
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra, which we shall call
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{K};D)
. We recall and generalize their construction and show that it agrees with the reduced groups obtained via Definition
5.21
if all of the
\mathcal{K}_{i}
are nonempty.

Given a non-empty compact Hausdorff space
K
, Emerson and Meyer define
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(K;D)
as the sub-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of
\mathrm{C}(K;{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D))
consisting of those functions for which
f(x)-f(y)\in D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}
for all
x,y\in K
.
For the empty space, we deviate and simply define
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(\varnothing;D)\coloneqq\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}}
.
If
\mathcal{K}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}
is a
\sigma
-compact Hausdorff space, then we obtain the
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra

\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{K};D)\coloneqq\big\{f\colon\mathcal{K}\to{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)\mid\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon f|_{K_{n}}\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(K_{n};D)\big\}=\varprojlim_{n}\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(K_{n};D).

Now we generalize it to locally compact Hausdorff spaces
X
decomposed into compact subsets
K_{i}
, which are allowed to be empty. To this end, for each
f\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(K;D)
we let
\Var(f)\coloneqq\sup_{x,y\in K}\|f(x)-f(y)\|
if
K\not=\varnothing
and
\Var(f)\coloneqq 0
if
K=\varnothing
.
Then we define

\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\coloneqq\bigg\{(f_{i})_{i\in\pi}\in\prod_{i\in\pi}\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(K_{i};D)\bigg|\big(\pi\mapsto[0,\infty),i\mapsto\Var(f_{i})\big)\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\pi)\bigg\},

recalling that we also consider
\pi
as a discrete space.
Note that it can be identified as a sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of
\mathrm{C}_{b}(X;{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D))
if all
K_{i}
are non-empty.

For the
\sigma
-locally compact space
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
decomposed as above into the
\mathcal{K}_{i}
we then define the
\sigma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra

\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{X};D)\coloneqq\varprojlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\mathrm{red}}(X_{n};D),

where the locally compact subspaces
X_{n}
of the filtration of
\mathcal{X}
are decomposed into the compact subsets
X_{n}\cap\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Note that it is even a
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra if
\mathcal{X}
is a
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-space and the decomposition is compatible with the
\Gamma
-action as in Section
5.4
.

The purpose of this cryptic construction is to have the short exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{X};D)\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{X};D)\to\frac{\prod_{\pi}{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)}{\bigoplus_{\pi}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})}\to 0

(6.1)

of
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras.

Lemma 6.4
.

The reduced groups of the cohomology theories
\mathrm{K}^{*}(-;D)
,
\mathrm{K}_{\Gamma}^{*}(-;D)
and
\mathrm{K}_{-*}(\mathrm{C}(-;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\rtimes\Gamma)
are canonically naturally isomorphic to
\mathrm{K}_{-*}\big(\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(-;D)\big)
,
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{-*}\big(\Gamma,\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(-;D)\big)
and
\mathrm{K}_{-*}\big(\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(-;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\rtimes\Gamma\big)
, respectively, in such a way that the corresponding sequences (
5.3
) are isomorphic to the long exact sequences induced by (
6.1
).

Proof.

We reuse the proof of Lemma
4.20
, but this time with the
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
I=\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{X};D)\allowbreak\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}
,
A=\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{X};D)
,
B\coloneqq\prod_{\pi}{\mathcal{M}}^{s}(D)
and
J\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\pi}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})
. Note that here we have

\Cone\biggl(\iota^{\prime}\colon\bigoplus_{\pi}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{X};D)\biggr)\cong\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}\mathcal{X};D)\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}

and that the
*
-homomorphism
\Cone(\iota^{\prime})\to\bigoplus_{\pi}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})=\mathrm{C}_{0}(\pi;D)\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}
is exactly the evaluation at the apices of the cones. The claim follows.
∎

The
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse
\mathrm{K}
-theory is the target of the so-called coassembly map, which we recall next. Let
X
be a proper metric isometric
\Gamma
-space. Then for any discretization
X^{\prime}
as in Lemma
2.26
, all the Rips complexes
P_{n}(X^{\prime})=P_{E_{n}}(X^{\prime})
carry an obvious
\Gamma
-isocoarse coarse structure compatible with the topology such that all of the inclusions
X\supset X^{\prime}\subset P_{m}(X^{\prime})\subset P_{n}(X^{\prime})
for
m\leq n
are coarse equivalences. It follows that
\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D)\coloneqq\varprojlim_{n}\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(P_{n}(X^{\prime});D)

is a
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)
,
because all of the maps in the inverse system are isomorphisms.
Furthermore we define the
\sigma
-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra

\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D)\coloneqq\varprojlim_{n}\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(P_{n}(X^{\prime});D)
.
By
[
10
]
they fit into a short exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D)\to\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D)\cong\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\to 0.

If
A\subset X
is a closed subspace and a suitable pair of discretizations
(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
has been chosen, then we obtain analogously the short exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});D)\to\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)\to 0,

where
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});D)\coloneqq\ker(\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D)\to\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});D))
.

Definition 6.5
.

The connecting homomorphisms in
\mathrm{K}
-theory

\displaystyle\mu^{*}\colon\quad\,\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\to\mathrm{KX}^{*}(X;D),

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mu^*\colon\quad\,}\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{KX}_{\phantom{\Gamma}}^{*}(X,A;D),

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{top}}^{*}\colon\ \mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}\big(\Gamma;\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X;D),

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mu_{\mathrm{top}}^*\colon\ }\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}(\Gamma;\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X,A;D),

\displaystyle\mu_{\rtimes}^{*}\colon\ \ \,\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\rtimes\Gamma\big)\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X;D),

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mu_\rtimes^*\colon\ \ \, }\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)\rtimes\Gamma)\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X,A;D),

associated to the above short exact sequences are all called
co-assembly maps
.

The three co-assembly maps in the absolute case have originally been defined in
[
10
,
11
,
14
]
, respectively.

It is usually
\mu_{\mathrm{top}}^{*}
which is of interest, because it is known to be an isomorphism in many important cases, whereas not much can be said about
\mu_{\rtimes}^{*}

[
12
, p. 72]
.
According to
[
14
, Lemma 5.2]
, the co-assembly map
\mu_{\mathrm{top}}^{*}
factors through
\mu_{\rtimes}^{*}
in the absolute case, but the same also holds true for the corresponding relative versions. Therefore, if one is only interested in surjectivity (as was the case in
[
14
]
), then
\mu_{\rtimes}^{*}
is good enough.

Theorem 6.6
.

The transgression maps associated to the cohomology theories
\mathrm{K}^{*}(-,D)
,
\mathrm{K}_{\Gamma}^{*}(-,D)
and
\mathrm{K}_{-*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(-;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\rtimes\Gamma)
factor through the assembly maps as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{K}}^{*-1}(\partial X;D)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\to\mathrm{KX}^{*}(X;D),

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}^{*-1}(\partial X,\partial A;D)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{KX}^{*}(X,A;D),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{K}}_{\Gamma}^{*-1}(\partial X;D)\to\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}\big(\Gamma;\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\big)\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X;D),

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{\Gamma}^{*-1}(\partial X,\partial A;D)\to\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{1-*}(\Gamma;\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X,A;D),

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(\partial X;D)\rtimes\Gamma\big)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}\big(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\rtimes\Gamma\big)\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X;D),

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X\setminus\partial A;D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\rtimes\Gamma)\to\mathrm{K}_{1-*}(\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)\rtimes\Gamma)\to\mathrm{KX}_{\Gamma}^{*}(X,A;D).

Thus, if a transgression map is an isomorphism
(
e.g., because
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
is contractible or
\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})}
is a deformation retract of
\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})})
, then the corresponding co-assembly map is surjective.

Proof.

The claims follow directly from the commutative diagrams with exact rows

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})};D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{red}}(\partial X;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime});D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{red}}(X;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\overline{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})};D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X\setminus\partial A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime});D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{c}(X,A;D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

which exist because functions on a Higson-dominated compactification restrict to functions of vanishing variation.
∎

6.4
Coarse
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}
-homology and assembly

Using
\Gamma
-equivariant
\mathrm{E}
-theory,
14
14

14

Of course,
\mathrm{KK}
-theory could also be used. In that case, a different description of the assembly maps would need to be used and hence completely new proofs of our results would be required. However,
\mathrm{KK}
-theory suffers from similar technical shortcomings as
\mathrm{E}
-theory and therefore no advantage over our approach is expected. In the end,
\mathrm{E}
-theory was primarily selected because of the author’s greater familiarity with this theory.
we define the
\Gamma
-equivariant
\mathrm{K}
-homology with coefficients in a separable
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra
D
as the functors

\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(-;D)\coloneqq\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(-);D).

Note that this definition requires the function algebras
\mathrm{C}_{0}(-)
to be separable, so we only consider these functors on the full subcategory of
\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
of all
second countable
locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces.
In practice, this is only a minor restriction to the theory of Section
5
, because we are mainly interested in two special cases: First, Rips complexes of discretizations of countably generated proper isocoarse
\Gamma
-spaces of bornologically bounded geometry are always second countable and, second, their compactification with a Higson dominated corona is second countable, if the corona is second countable, i.e., a compact metrizable space. The latter is an ubiquitous assumption in applications.

Due to the well-known properties of bivariant
\mathrm{K}
-theory (cf.
[
15
, Chapter 6]
), the functors
\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(-;D)
are
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space homology theories on the above-mentioned category. Furthermore, as direct limits preserve exact sequences, they can be extended to
\Gamma
-equivariant single-space homology theories on the full subcategory of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mathsf{LCH}_{\Gamma}^{+}
consisting of all second countable
\sigma
-locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
by

\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{X};D)\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X_{n};D)
.

We want to compare the resulting transgression maps with the coarse assembly maps
\mu
:
\mathrm{KX}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X;D)\to\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))
. The definition of the latter is based upon other pictures of
\mathrm{K}
-homology.
We choose the one using Yu’s localization algebras
[
47
]
(see also
[
32
]
and
[
14
]
) for our discussion.
A lot about localization algebras has been proven in the comprehensive book
[
41
]
, but note that the “localized Roe algebras” considered there are slightly bigger than Yu’s original localization algebras and hence a bit of care has to be taken when applying their results.

Following up our Section
4.4
about Roe algebras, let
X
be a proper metric space equipped with a proper isometric
\Gamma
-action. We assume that sufficiently large
\Gamma
-
X
-module
(H,\rho,u)
has been chosen and let
\mathfrak{H}\coloneqq H\otimes D
be equipped with the induced representations. Recall that we defined
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
as the norm closure in
{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{H})
of the
*
-subalgebra of all
\Gamma
-equivariant locally compact operators of finite propagation, and if
A
is a subspace of
X
, then
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)
denoted the ideal generated by all operators which are supported in an
R
-neighborhood of
A
for some
R>0
and
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)/\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)
.

Definition 6.7
.

The
localization algebra

\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)
is the norm closure of the sub-
*
-algebra of
\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty),\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))
of all bounded uniformly continuous functions
L\colon[1,\infty)\to\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
such that the propagation of
L(t)
is finite for all
t\geq 1
and converges to zero as
t\to\infty
. Furthermore, we denote by
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)\subset\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)
the ideal generated by all
L
for which there is a function
\varepsilon\colon[1,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
with
\varepsilon(t)\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}0
such that
L(t)
is supported in an
\varepsilon(t)
-neighborhood of
A
for all
t\geq 1
, and we define
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X,A;D)\coloneqq\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)/\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)
.

An obvious variant of
[
41
, Lemma 6.3.6]
, which can be proven with the same methods, says that the inclusion
A\subset X
induces via the theory of covering isometries an isomorphism
\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(A;D))\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(A\subset X;D))
. Therefore we obtain a long exact sequence

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(A;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D))\to\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X,A;D))

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{K}_{*-1}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(A;D))\to\cdots.

(6.2)

The relation between localization algebras and our definition of
\mathrm{K}
-homology is the following. As
{\mathfrak{K}}(\mathfrak{H})\cong D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)
, it is straightforward to verify (cf.
[
32
, Corollary 4.2]
) that

\displaystyle\delta\colon\ \mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)

\displaystyle\to\frac{\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty),D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{\mathrm{C}_{0}([1,\infty),D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))},

\displaystyle L\otimes f

\displaystyle\mapsto[t\mapsto L(t)\circ(\rho(f)\otimes\id_{D})]

defines an
\Gamma
-equivariant asymptotic morphism. It vanishes on the ideal
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(A\subset X;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(X\setminus A)
and hence we also obtain an induced
\Gamma
-equivariant asymptotic morphism

\delta\colon\ \mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X,A;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(X\setminus A)\to\frac{\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty),D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{\mathrm{C}_{0}([1,\infty),D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}.

Both of them define canonical
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-theory elements in
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{0}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)
and
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{0}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X,A;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(X\setminus A),D)
, respectively, which we shall also denote by the letter
\delta
.
15
15

15

Here we need
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-theory also for non-separable
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras. It can be defined in an ad hoc way as in
[
44
, Section 1]
, or even simpler by letting
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(A,B)
for possibly non-separable
A
,
B
be obtained from the groups
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
by taking the direct limit over all separable
B^{\prime}\subset B
and the inverse limit over all separable
A^{\prime}\subset A
. The relevant maps considered in the remainder of this section will be the same for both versions.
Note that these generalizations retain the composition and tensor products of
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}
-theory, but be aware that they are simply too naive to preserve the long exact sequences.

We obtain the composed group homomorphism

\displaystyle\Delta\colon\ \mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{E}_{*}(\mathbb{C},\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{-\otimes\id_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}}\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(X))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\delta\circ-}\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)=\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X;D)

and similarily
\Delta\colon\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X,A;D))\to\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X,A;D)
in the relative case.
By exploiting the homological properties of domains and targets of the maps
\Delta
(in particular that the domains are functorial under uniformly continuous coarse
\Gamma
-maps via the theory of covering isometries, that the maps
\Delta
are natural under such maps and that the connecting homomorphisms are in both cases constructed via mapping cones) we see that the maps
\Delta
map (
6.2
) to the long exact sequence of
\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(-,D)
.

Theorem 6.8
.

The maps
\Delta
are isomorphisms in the absolute case for every proper metric space
X
equipped with a proper isometric
\Gamma
-action and hence by a five lemma argument also in the relative case.
16
16

16

Instead of Theorem
6.8
