, preprint versions of this article contained the “Assumption 6.8” that
\Gamma
and
D
have been chosen for which the statement of this theorem holds. Thanks to a referee’s hint, we can now prove it in full generality.

Proofs of some special cases of the theorem have been known for a long time.
In the non-equivariant case (
\Gamma=1
) without coefficients (
D=\mathbb{C}
), one proof is sketched in
[
41
, Construction 6.7.5, Exercises B.3.2 and B.3.3]
, albeit for a different version of the localization algebra. Note, however, that this proof relies solely on homological properties and a comparison on one-point spaces. Therefore, it goes through for original version of the localization algebras, too, and even with arbitrary coefficient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
D
.

Another proof for the case
\Gamma=1
and
D=\mathbb{C}
can be found in
[
32
, Proposition 4.3]
and relies on Paschke duality. The author is indebted to a referee for pointing out a version of Paschke duality for the equivariant
\mathrm{KK}
-theory groups
\mathrm{KK}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)
which allows to prove the theorem in full generality.
Nevertheless, as localization algebras and
\mathrm{E}
-theory are defined in a very similiar manner, it would be interesting to know if the theorem can be proven directly without taking the detour via Paschke duality.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the theorem for separable
D
, because the general case follows by taking direct limits over all separable sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras.
In this case, we can make use of the Paschke duality for equivariant
\mathrm{KK}
-theory that was discussed in
[
16
, Appendix B]
.

An adjointable operator
T
on
\mathfrak{H}
is called pseudolocal if the commutators
\rho(f)T-T\rho(f)
are compact for all
f\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
.
Let
\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
denote the sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of
{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{H})
generated by the
\Gamma
-equivariant pseudolocal operators that have finite propagation and let
\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)
denote the sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of
\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty),\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))
of all bounded uniformly continuous functions
L\colon[1,\infty)\to\mathrm{D}^{*}(X;D)
such that the propagation
L(t)
is finite for all
t\geq 1
and converges to zero as
t\to\infty
.

We are free to choose a very specific sufficiently large
\mathfrak{H}=H\otimes D
, namely
\mathfrak{H}=\mathfrak{H}_{D}\coloneqq H^{\prime}\otimes\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})\otimes\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\otimes D
for a non-degenerate
\Gamma
-
X
-module
(H^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},u^{\prime})
.
It will be equipped with
\Gamma
-action
\varepsilon
which acts diagonally on the first, third and fourth factor and the representation
\pi
of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
which acts on the first factor.
For this choice,
[
16
, Appendix B]
contains the proof of isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}_{p}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)\cong\mathrm{K}_{p+1}\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)}{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)}\biggr)\cong\mathrm{K}_{p+1}\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)}{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)}\biggr)\cong\mathrm{K}_{p}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D))

for separable
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
D
and proper metric spaces
X
equipped with a proper cocompact isometric
\Gamma
-action.

The cocompactness assumption can be removed by performing slight modifications to the aforementioned proof: Instead of
\Gamma
-propagation we only use metric propagation (cf.
[
16
, items (ii) and (iii) on p. 74]
; in the co-compact case, finite
\Gamma
-propagation of a
\Gamma
-equivariant operator is equivalent to finite metric propagation anyway).
Furthermore, the proof utilises a cut-off function
c
, i.e., a compactly supported continuous function
X\to[0,1]
such that
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c(x\cdot\gamma)^{2}=1
for all
x\in X
. If the action is not co-compact, then we have to use the weaker condition on the support of
c
that it intersect only finitely many
\Gamma
-translates of each compact subset. The existence of such a cut-off function follows easily from the properness of the action.
With these modifications, the calculations in the proof of
[
16
, Lemma B.7]
go through. Just at the end of that proof one has to perform another truncation a la
[
22
, Sublemma 12.3.3]
by an equivariant partition of unity to turn the operator with finite
\Gamma
-propagation into one with finite metric propagation.
As
[
16
, Lemmas B.14, B.15 and B.16]
generalize to the non-cocompact case without any problems, the abovementioned isomorphisms follow.

Let us briefly recall the definition of the abovementioned isomorphisms in degree
p=0
.
According to the analogue of
[
16
, Lemma B.7]
in even degree
17
17

17

The cited lemma only considers the odd case, but the even case can be proven completely analogously.
, every class in
\mathrm{KK}_{*}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)
can be represented by a cycle of the form
\left(\mathfrak{H}_{D}\oplus\mathfrak{H}_{D},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&U^{*}\\
U&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\!,\epsilon\oplus\epsilon,\pi\oplus\pi\right)
with
U\in\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
.
Then
U
is a unitary modulo
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
and the Paschke duality isomorphism is defined by mapping this cycle to the class represented by
U
. The second isomorphism then takes it to the class represented by a function
t\mapsto U_{t}
which represents a unitary in
\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)/\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)
and such that
U_{0}
agrees with
U
modulo
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
. Such a function can be constructed from the constant function
U
by applying the procedure from the proof of
[
16
, Lemma B.15]
.
The third isomorphism then maps it to the class represented by the difference of the projection valued functions
t\mapsto P_{t}
and
t\mapsto Q
defined by

P_{t}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}U_{t}U_{t}^{*}&U_{t}(\id-U_{t}^{*}U_{t})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
U_{t}^{*}(\id-U_{t}U_{t}^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}&\id-U_{t}^{*}U_{t}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad Q=\begin{pmatrix}\id&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\!,

cf.
[
22
, Proposition 4.8.10]
.

In order to prove the claim in even degrees, it now suffices to show that
\Delta
composed with these isomorphisms is the canonical natural isomorphism
\mathrm{KK}_{0}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{E}_{0}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)
.
The latter is defined via
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
and hence we have to verify two properties: That the composition is natural in
D
and that it maps
1_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}\in\mathrm{KK}_{0}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),\mathrm{C}_{0}(X))
to
1_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}\in\mathrm{E}_{0}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),\mathrm{C}_{0}(X))
. Naturality is clear from the description of the three isomorphisms above and the definition of
\Delta
.

For the verification of the other property, recall that
1_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}\in\mathrm{KK}_{0}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),\mathrm{C}_{0}(X))
is represented by the cycle
(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)\oplus 0,0,\varepsilon^{\prime}\oplus 0,\pi^{\prime}\oplus 0)
, where
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime}
denote the canonical representations of
\Gamma
and
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
. After adding the degenerate cycle
\left(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}\oplus\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\id\\
\id&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\!,\epsilon\oplus\epsilon,\pi\oplus\pi\right)
, we see that it is also represented by the cycle
\left(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}\oplus\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&U^{*}\\
U&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\!,\epsilon\oplus\epsilon,\pi\oplus\pi\right)
for a projection
U\colon\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}\cong\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)\oplus\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}\to\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}
onto the second summand. Since
U
is a module homomorphism, it has propagation zero and
U\in\mathrm{D}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;\mathrm{C}_{0}(X))
. Furthermore we have
UU^{*}=\id
and
p\coloneqq\id-U^{*}U
is a projection onto an isomorphically embedded copy of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
in
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}
.
For the calculation of the image of this class in
\mathrm{E}_{0}^{\Gamma}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(X),D)
we can choose the constant function
t\mapsto U_{t}\coloneqq U
. We end up with the element that is represented by the
*
-homomorphism
\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)\to{\mathfrak{K}}(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)})
,
f\mapsto\pi(f)p
, and this is exactly
1_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)}
.

The isomorphism in odd degrees now follows from the one in even degrees by replacing
D
with the suspension
D\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.
∎

The reason why we do not just choose the
\mathrm{K}
-theory of the localization algebras as definition of
\Gamma
-equivariant
\mathrm{K}
-homology with coefficients is that we prefer to have a definition that works also for non-proper group actions, but the proofs of the homological properties of
\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(-;\mathbb{C}))
in
[
41
]
, in particular the existence of the all important covering isometries underlying functoriality, rely heavily on properness.
Note also that the equivariant Paschke duality that we used in the proof of Theorem
6.8
works only for proper
\Gamma
-actions, so the two definitions might truely be different in the non-proper case.
Unfortunately, this makes the proof of Theorem
6.12
below much more complicated than the argument using Paschke duality which was presented in
[
22
, Remark 12.3.8]
for the non-equivariant case without coefficients.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
(X,A)
be a pair of proper metric spaces equipped with a proper isometric
\Gamma
-action.
The(
uncoarsified
)
coarse assembly maps

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu\colon\ \mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X;D)\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D))\xrightarrow{(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}}\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)),\\
&\mu\colon\ \mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X,A;D)\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X,A;D))\xrightarrow{(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}}\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D))\end{split}

are induced by the evaluation at one.

The coarsified coarse assembly map will be obtained by taking the direct limit over all the uncoarsified assembly maps of the Rips complexes
(P_{n}(X^{\prime}),P_{n}(A^{\prime}))
of a discretization
(X^{\prime},A^{\prime})\subset(X,A)
.
As we have chosen a description of the assembly maps which requires proper metrics, we can’t content ourselves with the canonical topological coarse structures on the Rips complexes, but we have to metrize them.
Most authors equip the Rips complexes
P_{n}(X^{\prime})
with the spherical metric from
[
33
]
, but they have the drawback that the inclusions
X^{\prime}\subset P_{n}(X^{\prime})
are in general not coarse equivalences if the original space
X
was not a path-metric space.
Therefore, we use the
\Gamma
-invariant metrics from the following lemma, such that all of the inclusions
X\supset X^{\prime}\subset P_{m}(X^{\prime})\subset P_{n}(X^{\prime})
for
m\leq n
are even isometric coarse equivalences.
For a similar approach to metrizing the Rips complexes, see
[
41
, Section 7.2]
.

Lemma 6.10
.

We can assign to each discrete proper metric isometric
\Gamma
-space
X^{\prime}
a
\Gamma
-invariant metric
d_{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
on
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
such that

\bullet

its restriction to
P_{0}(X^{\prime})=X^{\prime}
is the original metric
d_{X^{\prime}}
,

\bullet

its restriction to each
P_{n}(X^{\prime})
is a
\Gamma
-invariant
proper
metric inducing the already existing topology on the complex,

\bullet

for all
m\leq n
,
P_{m}(X^{\prime})
is coarsely dense in
P_{n}(X^{\prime})
,

\bullet

if
f\colon X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
is a coarse
\Gamma
-map between discrete proper metric isometric
\Gamma
-spaces and
X^{\prime}
is
\delta
-separated for some
\delta>0
, i.e., the distance between each two distinct points of
X^{\prime}
is at least
\delta
, then all of the restrictions

\mathcal{P}(f)|_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}\colon\ P_{n}(X^{\prime})\to\mathcal{P}(Y^{\prime})

are low-scale Lipschitz coarse maps. Here, by low-scale Lipschitz we mean that there are constants
L,\delta^{\prime}>0
such that the Lipschitz inequality

d_{\mathcal{P}(Y^{\prime})}(f(x),f(y))\leq L\cdot d_{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}(x,y)

holds for all
x,y\in P_{n}(X^{\prime})
with
d_{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}(x,y)<\delta^{\prime}
.

The last property is very useful when working with the localization algebras, because the direct description of the functoriality of their
\mathrm{K}
-theory using families of covering isometries (in the concrete version of
[
14
, Definition 4.29 and Proposition 4.30]
) is only available for uniformly continuous coarse maps.
Note that
\delta
-separatedness is never a problem, because all discretizations either already have this property by construction (cf. the proof of Lemma
2.6
applied to the entourage
E_{\delta}
) or can be thinned out such that they satisfy this property.

Proof.

For
R>0
, the
R
-spherical metric on a
k
-simplex is the path metric obtained by identifying it with the subset

S^{k}_{+}(R)\coloneqq\big\{(x_{0},\dots,x_{k})\mid x_{0},\dots,x_{k}\geq 0\wedge x_{0}^{2}+\dots+x_{k}^{2}=R^{2}\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k+1}.

On each
P_{n}(X^{\prime})
with
n\geq 1
we first define the auxiliary metric
d^{1}_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}
as the largest metric such that its restriction to each simplex is at most the
2n
-spherical metric.
This metric is clearly a proper path metric and it induces the given topology, but it may take the value
\infty
and is not yet the restriction of the metric
d_{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
that we are looking for. Also, note that
X^{\prime}
is
\pi n
-dense in
P_{n}(X^{\prime})
with respect to
d^{1}_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}
.
On
P_{0}(X^{\prime})=X^{\prime}
we simply define
d^{1}_{P_{0}(X^{\prime})}=d_{X^{\prime}}
instead.

Thanks to the choice
R=2n
, similar arguments to those in the proof of
[
33
, Proposition 3.4(iv)]
(note also
[
34
]
) show that the inclusion
X^{\prime}\subset P_{n}(X^{\prime})
is distance non-decreasing with respect to
d_{X^{\prime}}
and
d^{1}_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence there is a largest metric
d^{2}_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}
on
P_{n}(X^{\prime})
which is less or equal to
d^{1}_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}
and restricts to
d_{X^{\prime}}
on
X^{\prime}
. These new metrics have the property that the inclusions
P_{m}(X^{\prime})\subset P_{n}(X^{\prime})
are also distance non-decreasing with respect to
d^{2}_{P_{m}(X^{\prime})}
and
d^{2}_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}
for all
m\leq n
.

Using all of these properties, it is now readily verified that there is a largest metric on
d_{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}
on
\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})
such that all of the restrictions to
P_{n}(X^{\prime})
are less or equal to
d^{2}_{P_{m}(X^{\prime})}
and this metric clearly satisfies the first three properties on the list and is
\Gamma
-invariant.

Now, if
f\colon X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
and
\delta>0
are as in the forth item on the list, then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is an
S_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d_{X^{\prime}}(x,y)\leq n
implies
d_{Y^{\prime}}(f(x),f(y))\leq S_{n}
. Note that
\mathcal{P}(f)
restricts to a
\frac{S_{n}}{n}
-Lipschitz map from
\big(P_{n}(X^{\prime}),d^{1}_{P_{n}(X^{\prime})}\big)
to
\big(P_{S_{n}}(Y^{\prime}),d^{1}_{P_{S_{n}}(Y^{\prime})}\big)
for every
n\geq 1
and, therefore, if
x,y\in P_{n}(X^{\prime})
with
d_{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}(x,y)<\frac{\delta}{2}
, then
d_{\mathcal{P}(Y^{\prime})}(f(x),f(y))\leq L\cdot d_{\mathcal{P}(X^{\prime})}(x,y)
with
L\coloneqq\max\bigl(S_{1},\frac{S_{2}}{2},\dots,\frac{S_{n}}{n}\bigr)
.
∎

Definition 6.11
.

Taking direct limits over the uncoarsified assembly maps of the Rips complexes, we obtain the (
coarsified
)
coarse assembly maps
,

\displaystyle\mu\colon\ \mathrm{KX}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X;D)=\varinjlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(P_{n}(X^{\prime});D)\to\varinjlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(P_{n}(X^{\prime});D))\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)),

\displaystyle\mu\colon\ \mathrm{KX}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X,A;D)=\varinjlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(P_{n}(X^{\prime}),P_{n}(A^{\prime});D)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mu\colon\ \mathrm{KX}^\Gamma_*(X,A;D)}{}\to\varinjlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(P_{n}(X^{\prime}),P_{n}(A^{\prime});D))\cong\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D)).

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
X
be a proper metric space
X
equipped with a proper isometric
\Gamma
-action and let
\partial X
be a componentwise Higson dominated corona as defined in Definition
5.23
. Then the reduced transgression map factors through the assembly maps as follows:

\mathrm{KX}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X;D)\xrightarrow{\mu}\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))\xrightarrow{\nu}\tilde{\mathrm{K}}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\partial X;D).

Similarily, if
A
is a subspace of
X
and
(\partial X,\partial A)
a pair of Higson-dominated coronas,
then the relative transgression map factors through the relative assembly maps:

\mathrm{KX}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X,A;D)\xrightarrow{\mu}\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X,A;D))\xrightarrow{\nu}\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\partial X,\partial A;D).

In particular, if the transgression maps are isomorphisms, then the assembly maps are injective.

Proof.

We first prove the reduced version in detail. Write
\partial X\coloneqq\coprod_{K\in\Pi_{0}(X)}\partial K
and let
\overline{X}\coloneqq\coprod_{K\in\Pi_{0}(X)}\overline{K}
be the decomposed locally compact
\Gamma
-space obtained by taking the Higson dominated compactification
\overline{K}
corresponding to
\partial K
of each coarse component
K\in\Pi_{0}(X)
.
The map
\nu
is then defined as follows.
The restrictions of functions in
\mathrm{C}_{0}\big(\overline{X}\big)
to
X
have vanishing variation on each coarse component and hence also on the whole space, so they commute with operators in
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)
up to compact operators as in the proof of
[
35
, Proposition 5.18]
.
Thus, there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant
*
-homomorphism

\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\overline{X})\to\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)},\qquad T\otimes f\mapsto[T\circ(\rho(f)\otimes\id_{D})]

which vanishes on
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(X)
and hence factors through
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X)
.
Note that its restriction to
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Pi_{0}(X))
lifts to an
\Gamma
-equivariant
*
-homomorphism into
{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))
when
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Pi_{0}(X))
is considered as the sub-
\Gamma
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebra of
\mathrm{C}_{0}\big(\overline{X}\big)
via pull-back under
\overline{X}\to\Pi_{0}(X)
.
Identifying
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X))
with
\Cone(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\Pi_{0}(X))\to\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X))
and applying the
*
-homomorphism above slice-wise, we obtain a
\Gamma
-equivariant
*
-homomorphism

\delta^{\prime}\colon\ \mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X))\to\Cone\biggl({\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))\to\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)}\biggr).

The inclusion of
D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)
as a canonical ideal in the mapping cone to the right induces an isomorphism on
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-theory and hence we can define the map
\nu
as the composition

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{E}_{*}(\mathbb{C},\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{-\otimes\id_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X))}}\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X)),\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X)))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\delta^{\prime}\circ-}\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*}\biggl(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X)),\Cone\biggl({\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))\to\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)}\biggr)\biggr)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X)),D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))=\widetilde{\mathrm{K}}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\partial X;D).

The definition of
\nu
is functorial under
\Gamma
-equivariant coarse maps and thus it identifies with the corresponding maps
\nu_{n}
associated to the locally compact
\Gamma
-spaces
\overline{X}_{n}
obtained from
X_{n}\coloneqq P_{n}(X^{\prime})
by gluing
\partial X
to it. We have to show that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n};D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\Gamma}(X_{n};D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{n}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X_{n};D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathrm{K}}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\partial X;D)}

commutes and then the claim follows by taking the direct limit for
n\to\infty
.
This will be done by considering for each
x\in\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n};D))
the following diagram in the
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-category, in which all solid arrows come from
*
-homomorphisms and all dashed arrows from asymptotic morphisms or
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-theory elements:

\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(X_{n})\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes\id}
\textstyle{\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{C}_{0}\big(\mathcal{O}^{+}\big(\overline{X}_{n}\big)\big)\\
\downarrow\\
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X))\end{pmatrix}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes\id}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X))\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes\id}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n};D)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(X_{n})\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\delta\otimes\id}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n};D)\otimes\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{C}_{0}\big(\mathcal{O}^{+}\big(\overline{X}_{n}\big)\big)\\
\downarrow\\
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X))\end{pmatrix}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n};D)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X))\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}\otimes\id}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes\\
D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1]\otimes{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))\\
\downarrow\\
{\scriptstyle\Cone\left({\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))\to\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)}\right)}\end{pmatrix}}}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes\\
\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}\\
\downarrow\\
\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)}\end{pmatrix}}\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes\\
D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1]\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)\otimes D\\
\downarrow\\
D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)\end{pmatrix}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes\\
D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

The rows of the diagram are all of the form

J\to\Cone(A\twoheadrightarrow A/J)\leftarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes A/J

for different
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras
A
with ideals
J
. The arrows going upwards are also induced by
*
-homomorphisms of the form
J\to\Cone(A\twoheadrightarrow A/J)
. All the arrows marked with “
\simeq
” are isomorphisms in
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-theory, because they are inclusions of ideals with contractible quotient
\mathrm{C}^{*}\!
-algebras. Furthermore, the mapping cone in the middle of the bottom row is itself isomorphic to
\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)
and the maps leading into it then come from the inclusion of
(0,1)
into itself as the left or right half, up to orientation. Hence they represent the same element in
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-theory up to a sign.

Using
[
41
, Lemma 6.1.2]
, which clearly also holds true for operators on Hilbert modules, and the fact that functions on
\overline{X}_{n}
restrict to functions of vanishing variation on
X_{n}
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)\mathbin{{\otimes}_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{C}\big(\overline{X}_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\to\frac{\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty),{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)))}{\mathrm{C}_{0}([1,\infty),{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)))},

\displaystyle L\otimes f

\displaystyle\mapsto[t\mapsto L(t)\circ(\rho(f)\otimes\id_{D})]

is an equivariant asymptotic morphism.
It clearly restricts to
\delta
on the ideal
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(X_{n})
and also passes to an equivariant asymptotic morphism between the quotients

\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X;D)\otimes\mathrm{C}(\partial X)\to\frac{\mathrm{C}_{b}([1,\infty),{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))/D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{\mathrm{C}_{0}([1,\infty),{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))/D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}.

Applying these constructions slicewise yields the asymptotic morphisms
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
.
In particular, we note that
\delta_{2}
is in fact given by the family of
*
-homomorphisms
(\delta^{\prime}\circ\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{t}})_{t\in[1,\infty)}
and as such it is
1
-homotopic to the constant family
(\delta^{\prime}\circ\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{t\in[1,\infty)}
. Hence
\delta_{2}
and
\delta^{\prime}
represent the same
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-theory class.

It is clear that the diagram commutes.
The left column now is
\Delta(x)\in\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}((0,1)\times X_{n};D)\cong\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X_{n};D)
and the right column is
\nu_{n}\circ(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}(x)\in\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X);D)\cong\tilde{\mathrm{K}}^{\Gamma}_{*-1}(\partial X;D)
. The middle column represents an element in
\mathrm{K}^{\Gamma}_{*+1}\big(\mathcal{O}^{+}\big(\overline{X}_{n}\big)\times\{1\}\cup\mathcal{O}(\partial X)\times(0,1];D\big)
which is mapped to both
\Delta(x)
and
\nu_{n}\circ(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}(x)
in the obvious way. By definition of the connecting homomorphisms
\partial
associated to the pair
\big(\mathcal{O}^{+}\big(\overline{X}_{n}\big),\mathcal{O}^{+}(\partial X)\big)
via the mapping cone construction, this shows exactly that
\partial(\Delta(x))=\nu_{n}\circ(\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}(x)
.

The proof of the relative case works completely analogously but a lot simpler by showing that for each
x\in\mathrm{K}_{*}(\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n},A_{n};D))
a diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(X_{n}\setminus A_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes\id}
\textstyle{\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{C}_{0}\big(\overline{X}_{n}\setminus\overline{A_{n}}\big)\\
\downarrow\\
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X\setminus\partial A))\end{pmatrix}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes\id}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X\setminus\partial A)\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\otimes\id}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n},A_{n};D)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(X_{n}\setminus A_{n})\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n},A_{n};D)\\
\otimes\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{C}_{0}\big(\overline{X}_{n}\setminus\overline{A_{n}}\big)\\
\downarrow\\
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X\setminus\partial A))\end{pmatrix}}\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{L},\Gamma}(X_{n},A_{n};D)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\\
\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\partial X\setminus\partial A)\end{array}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Cone{\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))\\
\downarrow\\
\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)}\end{pmatrix}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{C}_{0}(0,1)\otimes\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H))}{D\otimes{\mathfrak{K}}(H)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

commutes in the
\mathrm{E}^{\Gamma}
-category, where
\Delta(x)
is the left column and
\nu((\mathop{\textrm{ev}_{1}})_{*}(x))
is the composition of the right column with the bottom row.
∎
